=====[PDF 12 | printed p. 11]=====
Abstract

The Quantum Chemical Topology Force Field (FFLUX) takes a novel approach to
molecular simulation. In its modern formulation, classic fixed-form valence poten-
tials are replaced by non-parametric regression models that mimic the total quan-
tum mechanical energy. Likewise the electrostatics are represented by high-order
multipolar interactions that converge to the quantum electrostatic potential. Alto-
gether, this approach aims to overcome the obstacles of classical force fields in ac-
curately modelling the energetics of complex chemical phenomena, such as ligand-

receptor interactions.

This line of research is still in its infancy, and many problems remain unsolved.
We discuss some of these hurdles, including the neglect of polarisation, the prob-
lems of non-parametric regression when extrapolating beyond the training set, and
the curse of dimensionality, which imposes an exponential growth of the models as
the system size increases. We then introduce two major contributions to the Quan-

tum Chemical Topology Force Field.

Our first contribution sprouts from the need to find an inexpensive exchange
indicator, and evolved as a qualitative study of the delocalization index, a magnitude
from Quantum Chemical Topology that accounts for the amount of electrons delo-
calized within two atoms. We showed that this index can account for the covalent
interaction between both atoms, and that represents a useful indicator of bond or-
der. The findings for well-known bonding patterns were found to be extensible to

uncommon compounds, allowing to explain some previously disputed situations.

Our second contribution is the introduction of a novel formulation of kriging
that implicitly incorporates permutational symmetry, allowing to reduce computa-
tional cost and avoid some nonphysical descriptions. The form of this predictor is
reminiscent of the monomial symmetrization employed in the theory of invariant
polynomials, and together with a heuristic algorithm allows to greatly reduce the
number of training points. We implement this procedure in the DL_POLY 4 molec-
ular dynamics package, and show its capabilities in optimisations and simulations

of some small molecules.

Chapter 1

Contextualisation

OMPUTATIONAL chemistry is a branch of science that employs computa-
tional power and the knowledge of physical laws to provide insight about
microscopic phenomena. Despite the difficulties (including the abused

commentaries from Paul Dirac [1] and Walter Kohn [2]), computational chemistry
is applied almost routinely in a number of fields, and its capacities are to such an
extent that a recent perspective considers the problem of “predicting accurately the
properties of a hypothetical polyatomic molecule”, posed by Léwdin in 1967 [3], solved

for most practical purposes [4].

However, the realm of computational molecular biology still poses several prob-
lems of paramount interest both technical and practical. This includes long un-
solved problems, such as protein [5] and RNA [6] folding, but also the modelling of
intrinsically disordered proteins [7] or ligand-receptor interactions [8]. In these sys-
tems, instead of methods based on quantum chemistry, the energy surfaces are ex-
pressed as simplified potentials that attempt to represent physical insight by means

of easily computable functions.

For a long time, it was thought that this problem is the result of insufficient sam-
pling [9]; in other words, that during a simulation, the system cannot appropriately
explore the full energy landscape, and thus the statistics are not well described [10].
However, this argument could no longer be held when, in 2008, the company David

E. Shaw Research produced a special-purpose high performance computer that was

19


=====[PDF 21 | printed p. 20]=====
20 CHAPTER 1. CONTEXTUALISATION

carefully designed for molecular dynamics simulations [11]. The analysis of several
very long (> 100 ys) simulations performed in this computer showed how proteins
drifted away from their native structures, thus showing how force fields poorly
model the physics of the system [12]. The authors concluded that:

In our view, it is probably more beneficial in the long run to focus on the
development of better force fields than on the development of sophisti-

cated methodologies for scoring structures realised in simulation.

A significant amount of work has been dedicated to produce novel force fields,
mostly through the perhaps incremental approach of including polarisation effects
[13, 14]. Moreover, in a very recent perspective by Teresa Head-Gordon [15], it was

claimed that:

Looking further ahead, force fields may undergo a more radical transfor-
mation, moving away from certain privileged functional forms selected
based on physical observations. Machine learning opens up the possi-
bility of simulating interaction energies and forces without any sort of
explicit functional form, or at least of automatically selecting functional

forms.

With the advent of deep learning [16], several very successful models have
arisen, for example for prediction of thermochemical quantities [17] or as full-fledged
force fields [18].

The Popelier group has been amongst the pioneers employing machine learn-
ing for molecular simulation, since more than a decade ago [19]. This approach, the
Quantum Chemical Topological Force Field (QCTFF, later on renamed as FFLUX)
employs a non-parametric potential based on knowledgeable quantum atoms [20],
that has a strong focus on employing multipolar electrostatics to provide an accu-

rate modelling of intermolecular interactions.

This thesis captures the work performed by the author in the Popelier group
during the 2017-2018 academic year, as a Master of Philosophy student. Given the


=====[PDF 22 | printed p. 21]=====
21

obvious interdisciplinarity of the environment, and the openness of the project (“ex-
tend the applicability of the FFLUX force field”), this research follows a path that
might perhaps look sinuous at first glance, but that has been conveniently directed

to explore new ideas while exploiting previous developments.

In Chapter 2, we provide a review of the relevant literature in the area of compu-
tational chemistry. Given the vastness of the field, the scope is limited to the topics
directly related to the research. This includes molecular dynamics, intermolecular
forces and polarisable force fields as general topics, as well as some more specific
matters: quantum chemical topology and machine learning. Finally, we also review

previous work done by the Popelier group.

In Chapter 3, we present in detail the architecture and formulation of the FFLUX
force field, including a careful derivation of all the mathematical expressions re-
quired for the calculations of atomic forces and a description of the parametriza-
tion. We also provide a critical assessment of some of the challenges that the project

will have to tackle before a significant upscaling can be possible.

In Chapter 4, we explore a magnitude from Quantum Chemical Topology, the
delocalization index. This quantity is analogous to a charge-charge interaction for
the exchange interaction, and has been analysed as a possible candidate for inter-
model interactions in FFLUX. It has also proved to play a leading role in under-

standing bond order.

In Chapter 5, we develop a new technique: symmetry-adapted Gaussian Pro-
cess regression. This method can be used to incorporate permutational symmetry
into the models, and allows to reduce the size of the training set. We study this ap-
proach in a number of simple molecules, and show how it can be used to optimise

geometries with particularly reduced training sizes.

Finally, in Chapter 6 we gather the main conclusions of this work, and outline a

set of future lines of work that might be taken by future students of the group.


=====[PDF 23 | printed p. 22]=====
Chapter 2

Literature review

OMPUTATIONAL chemistry is a multidisciplinary field that involves and
brings together chemistry, physics, mathematics and computer science,
among others. Any attempt to completely map such extensive territory is

deemed to be futile, and even ambitious projects, such as the celebrated Encyclopedia
of Computational Chemistry [21], are doomed to become somewhat obsolete shortly

after their publication.

In all awareness of this difficulty, we have attempted to reflect the state-of-the-
art of computational chemistry in the most sensible way possible. This review fo-
cuses on the branches of computational chemistry that are directly related to the
research described in further chapters, and try to balance the basic theory to the

latest advances. The structure of the text reflects these concerns.

In this way, section 2.1 attempts to connect the basic concepts of quantum chem-
istry with the simulation in terms of molecular mechanics potentials. This is done
by sequentially introducing approximations to Schrédinger equations until clas-
sical force fields are derived. Then, the inadequacy of the typical intermolecular
terms leads to a discussion of the theory of intermolecular forces in section 2.2, and
later a review of a particularly successful improvement on the classical formulation,

polarisable force fields, in section 2.3.

Once the basic scenario is laid out, we proceed to tackle the areas related to the

particular formulation of force field studied here. In this way we will introduce the

22


=====[PDF 24 | printed p. 23]=====
2.1. MOLECULAR DYNAMICS 23

field of Quantum Chemical Topology, a key line of research in our group, in section
2.4, and then explore some rudiments of machine learning in section 2.5. Finally, in
section 2.6 we will initiate a short review of the main milestones of the Quantum

Chemical Topology Force Field, that are to be extended in Chapter 3.

2.1 Molecular dynamics
2.1.1 Time-dependent Schrédinger equation

The realm of matter at the microscopic level is governed by a set of complicated,
and somehow perplexing rules, dictated by quantum mechanics. This concerns
mainly the world at dimensions close to 10~° m, although quantum effects have
been observed at much larger scales [22]. Any computational methodology that
aims to faithfully reflect the behaviour of matter at this level must be (to a certain

extent, as we will see) consistent with the laws of quantum mechanics.

In practice, quantum mechanics is not a scientific theory per se, but rather a frame
theory: a paradigm that includes several theories with slightly different conditions
of applicability. These theories constitute the most powerful and consistent phys-
ical framework to understand microscopic phenomena. However, we should also
note that quantum theory is still in development: for example, recent high-accuracy
measurements in the Hz molecule show that even the most accurate quantum cal-

culations still fail to correlate with the dissociation energy up to 10~? cm™! [23].

Fortunately, the systems treated in this thesis will fulfil two conditions that allow
to select the most appropriate theory. In the first place, the scales of energy will be
relatively low, around a few electron-volts; therefore, quantum field theory is not
required. In the second place, effects due to relativity will be mostly negligible, with
the only exception of spin. Accordingly, we can employ non-relativistic quantum

mechanics, where spin is treated in an ad hoc manner.

Within these conditions, the postulates of quantum mechanics! establish that

the deterministic evolution of a quantum system is given by the time-dependent

1There are several equivalent formulations of the quantum mechanical postulates. Here we use
the set presented by Dirac in ref. [24]


=====[PDF 25 | printed p. 24]=====
24 CHAPTER 2. LITERATURE REVIEW

Schrodinger equation:

in? &(1; t) = H®(r;t) (2.1)

In this expression ® is the wavefunction, a function dwelling in the Hilbert space
£?(IR°) of normalisable functions. This function represents the state of a system,
and contains all the information that can possibly be measured on this system. On
the other hand, H is the Hamiltonian, an operator that yields the total energy of the

system.

Equation 2.1 is similar to Newton’s second law in quantum mechanics. Given a
system that is in the state ;,(r) at time t, it is possible to obtain the state of the sys-
tem at any time ¢ + t’, simply by integrating Schrédinger equation. Neglecting the
epistemological challenge of the probabilistic interpretation of quantum mechanics,
or the measure problem, it turns out that the methods in quantum mechanics are

not very different from their classical analogues.

Difficulties arise, however, when one tries to study a molecular system. The

form of the Hamiltonian for an arbitrary molecule is, in atomic units:

A= — Vaya “hp 1V2- a

A B>A i j>i

(2.2)

a

Here, we employ the uppercase indices A and B for the nuclei, and the lowercase
variants i and j for electrons. The five terms are, correspondingly, the nuclear kinetic
energy Truc, the electronic kinetic energy Telecr the electron-nucleus interaction po-
tential V,., the nucleus-nucleus interaction potential Van and the electron-electron

interaction potential Veo.

In equation 2.2 there are three many-body terms that hinder its exact resolution
in analytical form. We remark that this situation implies no novelties with respect to
classical mechanics, where a problem involving three or more bodies is equally un-

solvable. However, problems in computational chemistry can be significantly more


=====[PDF 26 | printed p. 25]=====
2.1. MOLECULAR DYNAMICS 25

involved due to several factors, including the symmetry conditions of the wave-
function, the immense dimensionality of the problem for systems with a handful of

atoms, and the scarce margin of error that chemical accuracy” admits.

A numerical integration of the full Schrodinger equation is possible for small
systems, although usually performed by means of wavepacket methods [25, 26].
This has been done to study several simple reactions [27], although bigger systems
can be approached using simulations based on Feynman’s path integral formula-
tion [28]. We note that these methodologies are necessary when the nuclear quan-
tum effects are of importance, for example in tunnelling, which is known to be of

importance in DNA mutation [29] and in the mechanism of many enzymes [30]

Nevertheless, the most extended approach to tackle equation 2.2 is the Born-
Oppenheimer approximation, that separates the nuclear and electronic degrees of
freedom, effectively reducing the three many-body problems to one. Most quan-
tum chemistry textbooks, including [31] and [32], introduce the Born-Oppenheimer
approximation in a very simplistic manner, and even graduate-level texts such as
[33] provide a reduced approach. Given the importance of this approach, espe-
cially in the connection of practical molecular dynamics with quantum mechanics,

we provide a full derivation.

Let us assume for a moment that we can fix the position of the nuclei in a given
configuration, and obtain the electronic eigenvectors for this arrangement. In this
layout, it is possible to define an electronic Hamiltonian Hetec = Tolec + Vee + Vine +
Von, where the last two terms are no longer many-body operators. The eigenvalue

problem is thus:

Hetec¥k(1;R, t) = Ex¥ x(t; R, t) (2.3)

Here r and R represent all the electronic and nuclear coordinates respectively;
note that the electronic wavefunction depends parametrically on the nuclear coordi-
nates. The eigenvectors of a physical operator form a complete basis, and hence the

full wavefunction ®(r, R;t) can be expanded as:

Here C); is the non-adiabatic coupling operator, which is given explicitly by:

The treatment up to this point has been exact. The coupling terms mix the nu-

electronic wavefunctions. The adiabatic approximation passes by neglecting these
mixing coefficients to allow the separation of the full wavefunction in nuclear and
electronic terms. Initially, one might assume that C;, ~ 0 for any k # I, which is

usually known as the diagonal non-adiabatic correction:

dx1(R, t) 1

Assuming a real wavefunction (for which the second, vectorial component of

~ pl 2

Hence, the nuclear wavefunction is no longer mixed with the electronic eigen-

The definitive approximation implies also neglecting the diagonal coupling Cj,
which leads to the full Born-Oppenheimer approximation:


=====[PDF 29 | printed p. 28]=====
28 CHAPTER 2. LITERATURE REVIEW

In their original article, Born and Oppenheimer showed the feasibility of ne-
glecting coupling terms by means of a cumbersome expansion of the electron-nucleus
mass ratio [34, 35], which will not be reproduced here. We note that there are also
numerous systems where the adiabatic approximations fails because nuclear mo-

tion involves more than one Born-Oppenheimer potential energy surface

These non-adiabatic phenomena have been known for almost a century; an
early paper by Fritz London shows how simple processes, like the trivial charge
transfer Ar* + N —> Ar + N* cannot be accounted from an adiabatic point of
view [36]. Several systems of enormous scientific and technical interest cannot be
studied fully within Born-Oppenheimer approximation, including the vibrations in
graphene [37] or the photodynamics in biological systems [38], among others [39].

The validity of Born-Oppenheimer approximation will be assumed to hold true
for the rest of this thesis. However, even if the nuclei can be assumed fixed, there is
still a many-body term in the molecular Hamiltonian eq. 2.2: the sum of electron-
electron interactions. The treatment of this term and the obtention of electronic
wavefunctions is a very interesting problem by itself that will not be treated in
detail here; a comprehensive overview of the most common methodologies may
be found in [40]. We will assume these methodologies to belong to the working

know-how of computational chemistry.

2.1.2 Ab initio molecular dynamics

Our treatment of Born-Oppenheimer approximation has allowed to separate the
nuclear and electronic degrees of freedom, but the nuclear dynamics are still de-
scribed by a wavefunction. Nevertheless, it turns out that assuming that the nuclei
move classically is a very neat approximation, that gives rise to the fertile terrain of

ab initio molecular dynamics.

In ab initio molecular dynamics, the nuclei move on a potential energy surface

that is determined by the electrons. This potential energy surface is computed on


=====[PDF 30 | printed p. 29]=====
2.1. MOLECULAR DYNAMICS 29

the fly at every timestep employing ab initio computational methods. There are
two main approaches: Born-Oppenheimer and Car-Parrinello molecular dynamics,

which will be discussed with certain detail here.

In the first place we introduce the classical equations of motion for the nuclei.
We follow the approach in [33], recognising that the nuclear wavefunction, as a

complex function, can be written as:

iS (Rit) | (2.15)

Xi (Rit) = Ax (Rt) exp | HC
Here A; and S, are real functions that represent respectively the amplitude and
phase of the wavefunction. This equation can be inserted into the time-dependent

Schrédinger equation and obtain a pair of equations for the amplitude and phase:

aS; ar 1 ~VRAy

t+ Dong (VeAx) (VaSk) +L’ (Vis) =0 (2.17)

The equations above employ the nuclear index B to avoid confusion with the
amplitude function. In order to obtain a classical description of the nuclei, we will
apply the well-known correspondence principle [41], and impose that the classical

description will be recovered as i — 0. This yields the following expression:

aS,

a + Doms (VBS;,)* +E =0 (2.18)

However, these equations looks exactly like the Hamilton-Jacobi formulation of
classical mechanics [42], where S; represents the action integral, and the second and

third terms in the left hand side are respectively the kinetic and potential energy.

In this framework, it can be shown that p = V5,, and since the total energy is

conserved:

OS 0(VBSx) _ Op _ tot

=====[PDF 31 | printed p. 30]=====
30 CHAPTER 2. LITERATURE REVIEW

Therefore:

2°Ra(E)

Mp 5p

= ~VaVP° ({Ra(t)}) (2.20)

This is the equation of motion of the nucleus B under the potential induced
by the electrons in the molecule. We note that this equation is fixed to a single

electronic state and does not allow transitions between them °.

Equation 2.20 defines the basis of Born-Oppenheimer molecular dynamics, where
the forces on each atom are computed from an ab initio calculation. This method is
compatible with virtually every electronic structure method (even when analyti-
cal derivatives are not available, since these can always be computed numerically,
although this is hardly advisable for most practical cases due to the strong compu-
tational overhead), and has been applied in different contexts, from solvation [43]

to reactivity [44].

However, since the errors in the derivatives are approximately linear on the
wavefunction error, BOMD simulations require a rather tight convergence criterion
to produce meaningful forces [45], resulting in significant computational expenses.
It seems that this problem could be palliated if the wavefunction from the previous
step is used as guess, although this results in drifts in energy [46] due to the break-
ing of time-reversal symmetry. Some methods have been proposed to reduce this
problem [47, 48, 49], but their impact in the literature is rather recent, and small in

comparison with its alternative: Car-Parrinello molecular dynamics.

This approach was proposed by Robert Car and Michele Parrinello in 1985 [50],
and allows to avoid the tight convergence of the wavefunction while maintaining
most of the advantages of Born-Oppenheimer molecular dynamics. The importance
of CPMD is such that no modern treatment of ab initio molecular dynamics can be
complete in absence of even a brief analysis. In this line of thought, although we

will not make use of CPMD techniques in this thesis, we will succinctly introduce

3An alternative is Ehrenfest molecular dynamics, where the dynamics of the electrons are fixed
by solving the time-dependent Schrédinger equation; however, we have omitted this treatment for
conciseness reasons, and because its importance is reduced in comparison to the most extended
BOMD and CPMD. In fact, Ehrenfest MD reduces to BOMD if the wavefunction is restricted to be
the ground state eigenvector [33].


=====[PDF 32 | printed p. 31]=====
2.1. MOLECULAR DYNAMICS 31

the basics.

The main idea of this approach is to propagate, rather than optimise the wave-
function. The idea is perhaps clearer if we reformulate BOMD in terms of a La-

grangian:

Car and Parrinello suggested avoiding the minimisation of the wavefunction by

introducing a set of fictitious electronic coordinates:

Leowp({9},{9}R/R) =5 DO MaR + EY (ily)
B i (2.23)

— E({yp;}, {Rp}) + constraints

Here, ip; are the occupied orbitals (CPMD is conceived for one-electron theories,
such as DFT or Hartree-Fock), and the constraints include orbital orthonormality
or, if necessary, thermostats, barostats, etc. The electronic degrees of freedom are
propagated under appropriate conditions to ensure that the system remains in the

ground state.

A major role is played by the parameter y, representing a fictitious mass for
electronic degrees of freedom. On one hand, this parameter controls the adiabatic
separation of electronic and nuclear subsystems, preventing a transference of en-
ergy between them that might take the system out of the ground state. On the
other, 1 is directly related to the maximum allowed timestep [33]. For this reason, it
is necessary to find an optimal value for each simulation, and a significant amount

of literature has been devoted to study this problem [51, 52, 53, 54].

Applying the Euler-Lagrange approach to obtain the equations of motion, it can
be seen that these are very similar to the BOMD; in fact, they become the latter as
pz — 0.

Car-Parrinello molecular dynamics simulations are generally performed in pe-
riodic systems, employing plane wave basis sets and pseudopotentials [55, 56, 57].
They have been widely applied to all kinds of problems, including reactivity [58],

aqueous solutions [59] and sampling for spectra prediction [60].

2.1.3 Classical mechanics: force fields

Ab initio molecular dynamics provides a complete treatment of the electronic be-
haviour and has been used extensively. Nevertheless, its significant computational
cost has precluded the application to very large systems and/or very large simu-
lation times. This hinders the study of phenomena such as protein folding, or ion

passing through biological channels, of deep scientific interest.

In these systems, most of the effects of quantum mechanics can be reasonably
approximated using simple functional forms that capture the basic physical be-
haviour. For example, bond stretching can accurately be fitted to a Morse potential

[61]:

V=D. (1 - eB Res)’ (2.26)

This is shown in Figure 2.1 for the trivial case of Hz. If one refuses to treat
systems such as reactions and decides instead to focus on near-equilibrium ge-
ometries, it is possible to obtain an expansion of the energy in terms of physically

sensible equations. The most typical expansion has the form:

paramount, such as enzyme catalysis. For those systems it is possible to apply hybrid quantum
chemical/molecular mechanics methods, where a region of the system is modelled quantum me-
chanically, and the rest with a classical force field. We will not discuss QM/MM in this thesis,
although we refer to reference [62] for a review of its applications to biomolecules.

Bond stretching and angle bending are mostly treated by a Taylor expansion
around their equilibrium values; Morse potentials have been used less extensively
due to the increased computational expense of evaluating exponentials. In prac-
tice, most force fields also include cubic and quartic terms; for example, for bond

stretching:

34 CHAPTER 2. LITERATURE REVIEW

Voond = ky (r — req) +k (r- req)” +k (r— req) (2.28)

Torsional terms are usually described by a Fourier series that allows to properly
describe the energetic barriers to rotation, although some force fields rely only on
non-bonded potentials. Some other force fields also include special terms for “im-
proper torsions” the typical example being cyclobutanone, where the carbonylic

oxygen requires an extra term to ensure the sp” planarity [63].

The expansion in equation 2.27 contains only two-body terms, but it is also pos-
sible to include many-body terms, known as cross terms. For example, the MM3
force field of Allinger [64] includes the following coupling between bond stretching

and angle bending, which has been inherited by many current force fields:

Vbe-coupling = kyo [(Ri _ Ri, eq) + (Ro _ Ro, eq)] (6 ~~ 8eq) (2.29)

In the last years there have been several force fields that employ machine learning
non-parametric regression methods to obtain the valence potential of a molecule;
the FFLUX force field that will be minutely described in Chapter 3 is one of such
approaches. Similar concepts are the TensorMol force field, based in deep neural
networks [18], and a formulation by Tkatchenko and Miiller based in kernel ridge
regression [65]. A comprehensive review of this current of force fields, which would

soon become obsolete, is beyond the scope of this section.

As it will be argued in the following two sections, the bane of classical force
fields is a crude treatment of intermolecular interactions. The majority of force fields

include two terms for non-bonded interactions: dispersion and electrostatics.

The most celebrated dispersion expression is, beyond doubt, the Lennard-Jones

potential [66]:

g\12 a\é A B
Vis = 4e () -(R) | ~ RI RS (2.30)
The parameters o and € have a very neat physical interpretation: the latter is the

depth of the potential well, while the former is the distance at which the interaction

2.1. MOLECULAR DYNAMICS 35

is zero. It is also clear that the first term is a repulsion at very short range, while the

second term is attractive at a short range.

Another usual choice is the Buckingham potential [67], that tries to model the

R-"? with a more realistic exponential term:

Cc
Veuckingham =A exp(—BR) ~ R6 (2.31)

Both expressions are fairly old, although they have been demonstrated to be
quite successful and are widely employed in many force fields (including the prac-
tical totality of examples mentioned in this thesis). Their parameters are usually
determined from fits to crystallographic data, or other experimental measurements

such as densities.

A problem of the dispersion interactions is that the parameters are dependent
on the atom types being involved. Since a system containing N atom types would
require ~ N?/2 parameters, it is usual to assume that the cross interaction param-
eters can be obtained using a set of mixing rules; for example, the Lorentz-Berthelot

mixing rules for the Lennard-Jones potential assume that:

1

€aB = VEAAEBB (2.33)

The treatment of electrostatic interactions is generally more important. The ma-
jority of classical force fields assume that the electron distribution in a molecule
can be appropriately modelled by means of a handful of point charges, placed at
the atomic sites (and sometimes in another extra sites, for example the non-bonded
electron pair in TIP4P [68]). One of the first methods was proposed by Cox and
Williams [69], and is based in computing the molecular electrostatic potential (MEP)
¢° at a grid of points {1;}. The charges in the model potential !° are optimised by

There are several other alternatives that rely on similar principles, such as the
CHELP method, employing a Lagrange multiplier approach [70] and the CHELPG
method which combines the other two [71]. Especially important is the RESP (Re-
strained ElectroStatic Potential) method of Bayly et al [72], which generalises the
method of Cox and Williams to include several regularisation constraints that pre-
vent nonphysically large charges and reduce the conformational dependence of the
charges. This method has been employed in the widely extended AMBER force
field, and can be considered one of the most popular [63].

2.2 Intermolecular forces

2.2.1 Introduction and historical context

In order to understand the weaknesses of force fields regarding non-bonded inter-
actions, this section will be concerned with the theory of intermolecular interac-
tions. Ordinarily, general chemistry textbooks provide a very simplified picture of
these forces, that is limited to classify molecules as polar or non-polar and induce
what kind of interactions should the condensed phase experience. However, inter-

molecular forces are a rich and insightful area of study.


=====[PDF 38 | printed p. 37]=====
2.2. INTERMOLECULAR FORCES 37

The terminology of this field is better understood within a brief historical con-
text. The first insights regarding the presence of intermolecular forces can be traced
back to 1743 [73], where a treaty of hydrostatics points out that capillary action can-
not be explained only by surface interactions between molecules and the tube walls;

actually, it requires a general interaction, embracing all molecules.

The theoretical advances of Johannes Diderik van der Waals, through its cele-
brated state equation (which would award him the Nobel Prize in Physics, in 1910),
transformed the existence of intermolecular forces in a topic of general interest. In

van der Waals’ equation

(P + vr) (V —nb) = nRT (2.37)

the term b corrects the volume by taking into account the intrinsic volume of
the component particles, and a corrects the pressure by means of certain attractive
interactions between the particles (in fact, some authors have defined intermolecu-
lar forces as that force which gives rise to the constant a in van der Waals equation [74]).
In his Nobel Lecture, van der Waals [75] declared that I came to the conclusion that
the attraction of the molecules decreases extremely quickly with distance, indeed that the

attraction only has an appreciable value at distances close to the size of the molecules.

The early theories of intermolecular forces have named the three most impor-
tant contributions to long-range interactions. Initially, Willem H. Keesom theorised
that van der Waals forces between non-charged molecules arise as a consequence of
the electrostatic interaction between their dipoles [76]. Although symmetry seems
to indicate that the global interaction must be zero, Boltzmann statistics assign sta-
tistical preference to low energy configurations, creating an attractive force with

form:

2 24,2
a= — xe ig ME <kT (2.38)

However, this same argument yields attractive forces that vanish with increas-
ing temperature, which contradicts experimental evidence. Shortly after, Peter De-

bye [77] remarked that these electrostatic interactions could not be the only cause.


=====[PDF 39 | printed p. 38]=====
38 CHAPTER 2. LITERATURE REVIEW

On the other hand, the charge distribution of a molecule can change in response to
the influence of another molecule; in fact, in a first order approximation, the induced
moment m is:

m = af (2.39)

where « is the polarisability, that is in general a tensor magnitude. We note that
this is only the first term in a Taylor expansion, and there are many cases where

higher-order terms cannot be neglected [78].

According to Debye, one molecule would polarise another, and these would

interact shortly. The contribution to the total energy would be of the form:

g=—Ae (2.40)

We note that both Keesom and Debye assumed molecules with non-zero dipolar
moment, although they also introduced corrections for quadrupolar moments. This

can be done, in theory, for electrostatic moments of any order.

However, both Keesom and Debye contributions are still incomplete. In the
first place, there is no way to explain the van der Waals interactions in the noble
gases, whose electronic densities are exactly spherically symmetric. On the other
hand, in the liquid and solid states, arguments of symmetry should lead to a great

diminishing, if not cancellation, of the aforementioned forces.

These problems were recognised by Fritz London in his seminal 1930 paper [79]
[80], which presented a novel contribution to the intermolecular forces, the dis-
persion®, with a completely quantum-mechanical origin. London introduced these
forces using a toy example: two coupled harmonic oscillators that gave rise to an

attractive force with an asymptotic R-6 decay.

Altogether, these findings comprise the three main types of intermolecular forces:
electrostatics (known as Keesom forces), polarisation (known as Debye forces) and

dispersion (known as London forces).

5By analogy with optical dispersion


=====[PDF 40 | printed p. 39]=====
2.2. INTERMOLECULAR FORCES 39

2.2.2 Insights from perturbation theory

The insight reached by the early studies in van der Waals forces is, at least, impres-
sive; they were brought forth in the early days of quantum mechanics, and with a
knowledge of chemical structure that was barely influenced by Lewis contributions.
In this section, we will show that all those insights follow from the application of
perturbation theory to the wavefunctions of two molecules.

2.2.4 Distributed multipoles

The results presented in the previous section provide powerful insight about in-
termolecular interactions, as well as expressions that could potentially be included
in a force field for describing them. However, there is a main hurdle to apply the

multipolar expansion: it suffers from convergence problems.

The convergence criteria for the multipolar expansion can be traced back to


=====[PDF 46 | printed p. 45]=====
2.2. INTERMOLECULAR FORCES 45

Figure 2.2: Spheres of divergences in the water dimer. The multipole expansion is

convergent whenever the two spheres do not intersect.

r’ | ~! can be rewritten as:

equation 2.52, where |r —

1 1

jr—r| |A+a—B—b| (2.69)

Here we follow the notation of Stone in [81] that has been adopted widely in
the literature. A and B are respectively the position vectors of the nuclei A and
B, while a and b are the vectors r and r’ (representing the coordinates of elec-
trons 1 and 2) in the reference system fixed on each atom. Moreover, R = A —B,
which allows to rewrite the denominator as ||R+a— b||, and division by ||R||
yields ||1 + (a—b)/ ||R||||, which is essentially 1/(1+ x).

The series must converge for an arbitrary r and 1’, so it is usually analysed in
terms of the least favourable combination of values, this is ||a — b|] = |la|| + ||b||-
By analogy with the convergence criteria of the Taylor expansion for 1/(1 + x), it

follows that the series will converge if ||a|| + ||b|] < ||R|| and diverge otherwise.

The concept of sphere of divergence presented in Figure 2.2 is usually employed:
if the spheres defined by a and b intersect, this means that ||a + b|| > ||R|| and the
multipole series will diverge. This seems to indicate that the multipole expansion
will diverge everywhere, since a and b (i. e. rand r’) extend formally to infin-

ity. Nevertheless, in practice these variables extend barely beyond a certain sphere


=====[PDF 47 | printed p. 46]=====
46 CHAPTER 2. LITERATURE REVIEW

Figure 2.3: Distributed versus molecular multipoles in the benzene dimer. The di-
vergence spheres of molecular multipoles (in black) preclude the computation of
the intermolecular energies, but distributed multipoles (in red and green) allow

this computation.

enclosing all the nuclei, defining a practical sphere of convergence [86].

It is not difficult to imagine cases where the spheres of convergence can hinder
the study of intermolecular interactions, specially in moderately sized molecules. A
way to solve this problem is to divide the molecule into several regions and describe
each of these by a set of multipole moments; in this way, instead of a big divergence
sphere for the whole molecule, there are several small divergence spheres localized

in the atoms. This is displayed pictorially in Figure 2.3.

There are several methods to assign multipoles to the different regions of the
molecule. Here we present the Distributed Multipole Analysis (DMA) method by
Stone [87], although several other methods have been proposed [86, 88, 89, 90]; in
particular, in Section 2.4.4 we present an alternative based in the quantum theory

of atoms in molecules (QTAIM), that will be used in this thesis.


=====[PDF 48 | printed p. 47]=====
2.2. INTERMOLECULAR FORCES 47

Let us first assume that we have chosen a set of sites in the molecule, and de-
velop how to assign the multipole moments to that particular region. The practical
totality of electronic structure methods (one exception being, for example, explicitly
correlated methods such as [91]) are based on more or less creative combinations of

monoelectronic orbitals, that are generally expanded in a basis:

$i(ti) = D> crix (te) (2.70)

The basis functions x(r,) (where r, = r—R, and R, is the position where

If we are to obtain the multipolar moment indexed by / and m, then only the terms
containing R),(r — p) will yield a positive contribution, due to the orthonormality

of regular spherical harmonics.

The centre p varies with the product of GTOs, since it depends on the exponents
of the Gaussian functions. However, given a multipole moment centered at p, it is
possible to displace it to a selected site denoted by P. This is done by a formula
obtained from the definition of an atomic multipole and equation 2.74, that yields:

kT /14m)\ (1—m\]2
=F ES [Ca] BBrnma(-P) 78)

k=0 q=—k

Two questions remain to completely determine the theory: which sites are cho-
sen to harbour the multipoles, and which terms are allocated to those sites. The
first question is answered in a rather arbitrary way: the centres are usually atoms,
or small groups of atoms, although sometimes multipole sites are placed at bonds.

The second question is slightly more involved.

Equation 2.75 seems to indicate that multipoles can be displaced freely along
the molecule. In practice, however, displacement by a distance t multiplies every
term in the expansion by t!~*. If the displacement is large, higher multipoles will be
enhanced, creating unnecessary computational expenses and enlarging the sphere
of divergence of the multipole expansion. Therefore it is in the best of interests that

the displacement is minimised as much as possible.

The most conceptually simple method is the nearest site algorithm, that assigns
the multipole to the site closest to p. The divergence sphere thus has a radius equal

to approximately half a bond length, and this can be reduced by including further


=====[PDF 50 | printed p. 49]=====
2.3. POLARISABLE FORCE FIELDS 49

sites at the centre of the bond [87]. This method is the most extended.

An alternative is the Cumulative Atomic Multipole Moment (CAMM) method
[90], that is simply a generalisation of the well-known Mulliken method for atomic
charges. In this way, half of the multipoles are allocated to each of the atoms on
which the basis functions in the product are centred. To the well-known inade-
quacies of Mulliken’s charge method [93], it is added that large movements of the
multipoles are usually involved. In fact, it has been shown that it is usually worse

than the central-multipole description [87].

Given their Hilbert space formulation, DMA multipoles are quite sensitive to the
basis set, and can have significant problems with diffuse functions [87]. An alterna-
tive procedure, based on numerical integration rather than analytical expressions,

has been shown to remove this problem [94]

2.3 Polarisable force fields

2.3.1 The need for polarisation

The theory of intermolecular forces that we have just analysed leaves classical force
fields in a quite unfortunate position. From the three interactions obtained from
second-order perturbation theory, the electrostatics are modelled crudely (the charge
is merely the first-order term in a Taylor expansion of the energy), the induction is

absent, and the dispersion is simplified to a Lennard-Jones potential.

The accuracy of first-generation (also termed additive) force fields is quite limited
by this simplistic functional form. There is a significant body of evidence suggesting
that an inaccurate modelling of electronic distribution is the main source of errors,

regardless of imprecision in the valence and dispersion potentials [95].

A commonly echoed argument states that a single water molecule presents a
dipole moment of 1.85 D in gas phase [96, 97], but this increases to approximately
2.1 D in the dimer, further in large water clusters [98], and about 2.95 D in bulk
[99, 100]. However, classical MD simulations of the latter describe an average dipole
of 2.4-2.6 D [101, 102].


=====[PDF 51 | printed p. 50]=====
50 CHAPTER 2. LITERATURE REVIEW

On the other hand, since biomolecular simulations are one of the main appli-
cations of force fields, the poor modelling of electronic distribution is bound to be
important in protein folding studies, especially when hydrophobic aminoacids are
transferred from solution to the core of the peptide [103, 104]. More importantly, the
lack of polarisation effects is known to deteriorate the description of non-covalent
interactions [105], which can be of enormous importance if one attempts to design

a scoring function for docking [8].

Additive force fields are generally parametrized using a mean-field approxima-
tion: they respond in an average way to different environments. However, this
does not allow to describe a variety of systems, where a finer description of the
physical reality must be achieved to obtain the right answers for the right reasons.
The branch of polarisable force fields is based on the perhaps incremental approach
of allowing the electrostatics of a molecular system to vary as a response to the

environment.

In this section, we offer a review of the main literature in polarisable force fields
during the last two decades. We recognise the three major branches: fluctuating
charges, Drude oscillators, and inducible dipoles. Our review is far from compre-

hensive, and aims to contextualise rather than enumerate.

We will focus on the physical treatment of polarisation, and obviate other details
such as parametrization (which, in many cases can change significantly, even within
a branch) or applications. This information can be accessed in a number of reviews,
both of polarisable force fields in general [13, 103, 106, 107], and of the particular
classes [108, 109].

2.3.2 Fluctuating charges

The fluctuating charge model for polarisable force fields is perhaps one of the sim-
plest ideas: given the lack of transferability of fixed charges, these are allowed to
fluctuate on the basis of a physical mechanism. The latter is grounded on the idea
of electronegativity equalisation [110], that the charge in a molecule is redistributed to

equalise the electronegativity at each point.


=====[PDF 52 | printed p. 51]=====
2.3. POLARISABLE FORCE FIELDS 51

This approach was initially proposed by Rick et al in 1994 [111]. The authors

expanded the energy required to create a charge Q in the molecule in a Taylor series:

2

Assuming a neutral atom as reference, 0E/dQ can be computed by finite dif-
ferences, since the energy to induce a charge +1 is the ionisation energy IP and the

energy to induce a -1 charge is the negative of the electronic affinity AE:

B _IE+EA _ 19 (2.77)
Q=0

aQ 2

This is Mulliken electronegativity [112]. By similar arguments [103] it is possi-
ble to show that the second derivative is twice the chemical hardness [113] of the
isolated atom. We note, however, that these parameters are in general treated as
purely empirical [108]. Now, the energy of a system of M molecules containing

each N; atoms can be written as:

MN. 1MM[N MN
E=) Ye xR Qin + 5 YY | YE ¥ ainjgQinQip + Vne(Tinjp) (2.78)

i=la=1 i=1j=1 |a=1B=1

In this expression Vne(Tiajp) gathers the contributions to the energy other than
the electrostatic. Now, the idea of the fluctuating force fields is that, at each step, the
charges are modified in response to the environment. This is done by differentiating

equation 2.78 with respect to the charge on the site a on molecule i:

i=1,N;
OF go ford! i (2.79)
OQix a=1,M

This procedure leads to electronegativity equalisation: the charges that min-

imise the energy are those for which the electronegativities are equal. In general,


=====[PDF 53 | printed p. 52]=====
52 CHAPTER 2. LITERATURE REVIEW

constraints are introduced to ensure that charge is conserved within a molecule; re-
moving this constraint leads to a large overestimation of polarisability [106]. How-
ever, given the importance of charge transfer from a quantum mechanical point of

view, some models have been proposed to ease this problem [114, 115, 116].

One of the main advantages of the fluctuating charges method is that it intro-
duces no new potentials, and thus the implementation is easy and can take advan-
tage of previous software development. There are also efficient methods to propa-
gate the charges in an extended Lagrangian fashion [103, 108], although the smaller

timesteps required can burden computation nonetheless.

Nevertheless, the fluctuating charges method has some intrinsic problems. As a
start, the charges represent only the first moment in the multipole expansion, and
do not represent the electron distribution accurately in general [117]. In this way,
fluctuating charges force fields include polarisation corrections, but their treatment

of electrostatics is no better than in additive force fields.

There are also more technical problems. On one hand, it has been shown that
out-of-plane polarisation for planar or linear moieties is not reproduced, since the
charge transfer proceeds along the bonds [103]. Additionally, it was shown that
fluctuating charges cannot reproduce the energy of alanine tetrapeptide conform-
ers [115], which has prompted a migration of research to the induced dipoles frame-
work [103] described vide infra.

There are several current implementations of the fluctuating charge model. Some
of the most extended include the universal force field UFF [118], and a version of
CHARMM22 [119, 120], as well as the original TIP4P/FQ [111] model. There is also
a generalisation of the method to fluctuating multipoles [121].

2.3.3 Classical Drude oscillator

The classical Drude oscillator model owes its name to the work of the German
physicist Paul Drude at the beginning of 20th century. His Drude model tried to ex-

plain the electrical properties in metals by assuming that electrons could be treated


=====[PDF 54 | printed p. 53]=====
2.3. POLARISABLE FORCE FIELDS 53

as classical point particles that collided with the atoms in the lattice [122, 123]. De-
spite its simplicity, it provides powerful insight, and its properties are significantly

close to the real values, specially in semiconductors [124].

The Drude oscillator model attempts to mimic electronic polarisation by con-
sidering atoms as two-particle systems that are joined together by a spring with
constant kp. These two particles are referred to as the core atom, with charge q4 and
the Drude oscillator with charge gp (and g4 + 4p = 4q, being this the total charge of

the atom).

The separation of the Drude oscillator and the core (which we represent by the
vector d; for the ith oscillator) represents a dipole. At each step of the MD simu-
lation, the displacement of the Drude oscillator will be updated in a self-consistent

fashion to minimise the energy:

dE
5a, °C (2.80)

Let us take a given site i, and assume that the environment (including all other
sites) generates an electric potential ¢(r). The contributions of the energy at this
site that depend on the displacement are given by the harmonic potential at the

oscillator and the electric interaction:

E = qp($(1; + di) — $(ti)) + kp |||? (2.81)

All the induced dipoles have to be solved self-consistently, and this is usually the
limiting factor in simulations. Fortunately, extended Lagrangian approaches allow


=====[PDF 55 | printed p. 54]=====
54 CHAPTER 2. LITERATURE REVIEW

for important computational savings [125]; it is estimated that simulations using
Drude oscillator model are only about 4 times slower than comparable simulations
with classical additive force fields, and half of this difference is owed to the smaller
timestep [109].

One of the most attractive features of Drude oscillator model is that it employs
the simple potentials (harmonic potential for the spring, Coulombic interaction for
the charges) encountered in most molecular dynamics programs and therefore its

implementation is relatively straightforward [106].

We note that there are other flavours of Drude models; for example, the inclu-

sion of the multipole expansion to simulate ionic liquids [126]

2.3.4 Inducible dipoles

Inducible dipoles models are perhaps the most studied. In this approach, polarisa-
tion energy is described as the interaction between atomic charges (or, in general,

atomic multipoles) and the dipole moments that are induced.

The polarisation process is usually described employing a modification of the
Applequist model [127] to include Thole damping [128, 129]; such model will be
presented here. Let us take a site p, the induced dipole will be:

relatively straightforward.

latter, known as relay matrix, provides a generalised polarisability that in turn allows

to obtain the induced dipoles directly from the external field.

However, this method has a significant problem, known as polarisation catastro-

diminishes, their divergence spheres overlap and the multipole expansion ceases to

The main exemplar of inducible dipole force field is AMOEBA (Atomic Multi-
pole Optimized Energetics for Biomolecular Applications), developed by Ren, Pon-
der and coworkers [130]. The electrostatics in AMOEBA include permanent atomic
multipoles up to quadrupole, obtained from distributed multipole analysis, and

induced dipoles from the scheme discussed above.

This force field has been released for proteins [131], and very recently for nucleic
acids [132]. Its formulation has shown significant improvement over classical force
fields in fields such as protein folding, protein-ligand binding and refinement for X-
Ray crystallography [133]; however, its increased computational cost has precluded

an extended use [103].

Another example is the NEMO (Non-Empirical MOdel) [134], that employs a
description of the electrostatics very similar to AMOEBA, but including induced

quadrupoles as well as dipoles.

2.4 Quantum Chemical Topology

Since the start of this thesis, we have kept a strong will to connect our line of rea-
soning to the principles of quantum mechanics. However, there is a matter which
has not been addressed yet. Chemistry is built around a handful of very useful con-
cepts: the notions of atoms, bonds, and movements of electrons that allow to ex-
plain an enormous body of knowledge across the chemical sciences. Unfortunately,

connecting these concepts with quantum mechanics is hardly a trivial matter.

The notion of individual or discrete atoms has a long story, starting with the
early insights of Democritus. While the atomic theory had to strive for long before
it was totally accepted (let us note that several biographers have linked the suicide
of Ludwig Boltzmann in 1906 to the resistance of the scientific community to accept

the atomic theory), it is deeply engraved in the scientific thinking.

The concept of atoms, as the bond and many others, was developed previously
to the advent of quantum mechanics, and reconcile the discreteness of matter with
wavefunctions has been one of the classical conundrums of theoretical chemistry.
One of the main difficulties in this pursuit is the fact that, in quantum mechanics,

the only magnitudes that have a physical manifestation (i. e. measurable) are the


=====[PDF 58 | printed p. 57]=====
2.4. QUANTUM CHEMICAL TOPOLOGY 57

eigenvalues of an appropriate quantum mechanical operator: the so-called Dirac

observables.

O=(¥|Ol¥) = [ord (2.90)

A theory that allows to recover the division of a molecule in atoms, maintaining
the classical chemical vision while ensuring that all molecular and atomic proper-
ties remain as Dirac observables was proposed by Richard F. W. Bader in the sev-
enties, under the name of Quantum Theory of Atoms in Molecules (QTAIM). This
theory is based on the division of real space IR° in several atoms, which constitute

open quantum systems.

While not enjoying the same quantum mechanical rigour, other theories propos-
ing a partition of real space have been proposed, including the Electron Localiza-
tion Function (ELF) [135] and Hirshfeld partitioning [136], to name a few. These
theories have been widely considered to form part of a family of techniques called
Quantum Chemical Topology (QCT), a term proposed by Prof. Popelier in 2003, in a
quite extended footnote [137]. However, while the term was coined by Popelier, the
conception of these theories as topological analysis was well-extended in the commu-
nity before; proof of this is the presence of titles as “Classification of chemical bonds
based on topological analysis of electron localization functions”, the Nature paper
by Silvi and Savin in 1994 [138].

The name topology is a consequence of the atomic partitioning inducing a topol-

ogy in real space, in the usual mathematical sense [139]:

Definition 1 (Topology) A topology on a set X is a collection (i. e. a set whose elements
are sets) T of subsets of X having the following properties:

1. © (the empty set) and X are in T,
2. The union of the elements of any subcollection of T is in T,

3. The intersection of the elements of any finite subcollection of T is in T.

The different areas of Quantum Chemical Topology are each one a considerable


=====[PDF 59 | printed p. 58]=====
58 CHAPTER 2. LITERATURE REVIEW

subject on their own; for example, Bader’s book on QTAIM [140] has been cited
more than 16,000 times at the moment of writing. In this section, we will describe
the main features of QTAIM, which has been a main area of research in our group,
as well as the Interacting Quantum Atoms (IQA) energy decomposition scheme.
We also describe the definition of topological multipoles, which is of significant

importance in the research presented later.

2.4.1 The Quantum Theory of Atoms in Molecules

The Quantum Theory of Atoms in Molecules is a paradigm in molecular quantum
chemistry proposed by Richard F. W. Bader in the seventies and eighties [140]. The
central point to this theory is that it is possible to define a partition of real space
IR° by means of a set of interatomic surfaces (IAS), that are defined by the zero flux

condition:

Vp-n=0 (2.91)

The regions {O,,} defined by this partition are termed atomic basins. Their con-
nection to the intuitive concept of atom is made evident by the theory of dynamical
systems: the electron density approaches a maximum close to the nuclear positions
(mathematically there is a singularity, although in practice there is a real value).
Therefore, the nuclei act as attractors of the electron density, and the IAS are simply

the regions that separate the territory of two attractors.

More information can be obtained if one turns to the different critical points of
p(r), the electron density. A given critical point can be classified by means of the
eigenvalues of its Hessian, in terms of its rank w (number of non-zero eigenvalues)
and signature o (the sum of the signs of the eigenvalues). Since only the points with
w = 3 are stable upon small perturbations of the geometry, one can classify these

points as displayed in Table 2.1.
We note that this classification is perhaps misleading in some cases. For ex-
ample, it is not rare to find (3, -3) critical points in non-nuclear positions, the so-

called Non-Nuclear Critical Points (NNCPs); this is a feature typically encountered


=====[PDF 60 | printed p. 59]=====
2.4. QUANTUM CHEMICAL TOPOLOGY 59

Point type (w, 0)

Nuclear critical point (NCP) (3, -3)
Bond critical point (BCP) (3, -1)
Ring critical point (RCP) (3, +1)
Cage critical point (CCP) (3, +3)

Table 2.1: Classification of the critical points of the electron density

in triple carbon-carbon bonds, such as acetylene. For a long time it was believed
that these were a consequence of ill-posed calculations, but they were discovered
experimentally in the nineties [141, 142, 143], and later shown to be a natural con-

sequence of the bond distance decreasing by Pendas [144].

Of greater importance are the bond critical points. As their name indicates, these
were initially thought to be the fingerprint of a bond [145, 146], although several
criticisms [147, 148, 149] led Bader to retract his position in 2009 [150]. Today the

community is persuaded that BCPs are not equivalent to bonds [151].

The following step in our presentation of QTAIM is to define the concept of
atomic observable; to do this, however, we need to ensure that the systems defined
by the zero flux condition described above are quantum-mechanically consistent.
Following a recent preprint on the open problems of QTAIM [152], there are three
main ways of deriving this fact (and the zero flux condition): through the unique-
ness of the kinetic energy (however, it has been pointed out that not all families
of kinetic energy operators will yield the same expectation value if integrated over
a QTAIM basin [153]), through the atomic virial theorem and through the varia-
tional principles. All this methods are described to some extent in Bader’s original

monograph [140].

In this section, we have decided to employ the variational formulation for its
particular elegance. We note, however, that the use of Schwinger’s action principle
as done by Bader has been criticised by Cassam-Jenai and Jayatilaka due to math-
ematical problems [154, 155, 156]. Here, we follow the approach of Pendas et al.
[157], which is more mathematically elegant in our view, and does not suffer of this
problem, since it does not employ Schwinger’s action principle. For a mathemati-

cally rigorous derivation, we refer to the work of Shahbazian [158].


=====[PDF 61 | printed p. 60]=====
60 CHAPTER 2. LITERATURE REVIEW

This approach is based on the work of Schrédinger [159]:

2.4.2 Interacting Quantum Atoms

The Interacting Quantum Atoms (IQA) formalism is an energy decomposition scheme
based on an exhaustive topological partition of the space that was developed by
Blanco et al [160] in 2005.

The strength of this approach relies in the use of first- and second-order reduced
density matrices [161, 162], respectively abbreviated as 1-RDM and 2-RDM:

o1(1:1') = N, [ve ¥,(1,...,Ne)¥2(1',...,Ne)dxp...dxn, (2.106)

which was employed by the Oviedo group to derive the IQA formalism [160].

The importance of these matrices is that they contain all the information nec-
essary to determine any one- or two-electron property; for example, the expected

=====[PDF 67 | printed p. 66]=====
66 CHAPTER 2. LITERATURE REVIEW

Although we lack space to start a full comparison, we mention briefly that IQA
has several advantages with respect to other, more classical energy decomposition
schemes. As opposed to the celebrated Energy Decomposition Analysis (EDA) by
Morokuma [164], IQA does not require a reference state, which is desirable not only
from the point of minimality, but also when reference states can be problematic (for
example, chemical reactions). On the other hand, when compared to Natural Bond
Orbital (NBO) analysis, it can easily reflect long-range interactions that are obscured

by the localized treatment of NBO.

Unfortunately, computing some of the IQA terms is lengthy, since they require
six-dimensional integrations in an irregular boundary, that are computationally ex-
pensive due to the curse of dimensionality (see Section 3.4.3). Moreover, for a sys-
tem with N atoms, N(N — 1) such integrations will be required. Due to its elevated
computational cost, IQA is mostly employed by the theoretically-oriented commu-

nity, and has not spread as widely as other approaches.

2.4.3 Interacting Quantum Atoms and DFT

The IQA formalism is defined in terms of the first- and second-order reduced den-
sity matrices, which can be computed from the wave function. This can be done
easily for Hartree-Fock wavefunctions, and in general for any wavefunction based
on a expansion of Slater determinants (MCSCF, CI expansions, etc.). However, it is
not so clear how to do this when there is not a simple wavefunction, as in Mdller-
Plesset perturbation theory or coupled-cluster theory, or when directly there is not

a wave function, as in Density Functional Theory (DFT).

In this subsection, we explore the formulation of IQA in terms of Kohn-Sham
orbitals, which was initially presented by the Popelier group for B3LYP [165], and
then generalised by the Pendads group [166], and which will be used intensively
along this thesis. We also note that the Interacting Quantum Atoms has recently
been extended to MGller-Plesset Many-Body Perturbation Theory [167, 168], and to
Coupled-Cluster theory [169].

We remind that the Kohn-Sham approach [170] employs a density functional


=====[PDF 68 | printed p. 67]=====
2.4. QUANTUM CHEMICAL TOPOLOGY 67

having the following form:

E [pe] = T [e] + Vne [0] + Vee [0] + Exc [0] (2.122)

where Ex¢ [p] is the so-called exchange-correlation functional, which contains all effects
(for example the correlation of the electrons, or the inadequacy of the Kohn-Sham
kinetic energy functional) necessary to yield the exact energy. The electron den-
sity e(r) is obtained by an expansion in terms of monoelectronic functions that are

known as Kohn-Sham orbitals.

The problem posed by the Kohn-Sham method is that there is no way to calcu-
late a second-order reduced density matrix, as in wavefunction theory. However,
using the fine structure of the 2-~RDM described vide supra, we note that only the

exchange-correlation part is actually unknown:

P2(t1,42) = p(r1) (12) + Pxc(r1, 42) (2.123)

The problem of producing a reasonable adaptation of the Interacting Quan-
tum Atoms scheme is thus reduced to providing an appropriate partition of the
exchange-correlation energy. Before proceeding to do so, we establish a division
between hybrid and non-hybrid functionals, depending on whether they use or not

the HF expression for the exchange. For the last class, also referred as local:

EXt loc = J, 002) Exc [e] ar (2.124)

and, if the functional is hybrid:

EDFT _ I ; p(r) Ex. |p] dr + agESS (2.125)

where 4 is the coefficient of the HF exchange in the functional (for example, 0.2
in B3LYP [171]), and the prime in Ej, indicates that the HF exchange is not in-
cluded to avoid double-counting. Note that although we employ the ESS, we could
equivalently write E!"; we have chosen the former for consistence with the original

publication [166], and to emphasise that the exchange is calculated employing the


=====[PDF 69 | printed p. 68]=====
68 CHAPTER 2. LITERATURE REVIEW

Kohn-Sham orbitals obtained from a DFT calculation.

The partition of the total exchange-correlation is of heuristic nature. In the first
place, we simply apply the exhaustive atomic partition to the expression of ERFT
(we will assume an hybrid functional without loss of generality; the extension to

local is trivial):

Finally, we note that there is an ongoing discussion in the literature [172, 173,
174, 175, 176] regarding how density functionals seem to provide reasonable en-
ergy differences, but their description of the electron density (and thus the Kohn-
Sham orbitals) might be very poor. We note that, in absence of the exact functional
promised by Hohenberg-Kohn first theorem, there is no guarantee that the Kohn-
Sham orbitals will provide a good description of the electronic structure, and thus
IQA calculations might be completely unreliable. Nonetheless, their use is extended

given the enormous computational expense of ab initio correlated methods.

2.4.4 Topological multipoles

The partition of molecular observables in atomic contributions presented vide supra
provides a very elegant way to distribute the molecular multipole. More impor-
tantly, with the sensitivity on basis set that hinders the Distributed Multipole Anal-

ysis of Stone, a formulation in real rather than in Hilbert space results promising.

The simplest multipole is simply the integral of the electron density over the

atomic density:

Qh = - [, ee) dr° (2.131)

Here, the negative sign takes account of the negative charge of the electron.
This is the content of electrons of the basin, and cannot be interpreted as an atomic

charge; this requires to subtract the nuclear charge:

q4 =Za— QA (2.132)


=====[PDF 71 | printed p. 70]=====
70 CHAPTER 2. LITERATURE REVIEW

The rest of the multipoles are defined trivially, simply by applying the corre-
sponding multipole operator in equation 2.105. For example, the three components

of an atomic dipole are given by:

Quo = I o(r)C10(8,) dr? (2.133)
Que = I o(r)Cic(8,) dr? (2.134)
Qis= fi ple)crs(6,9) ar (2.135)

where C),,(0,) are the renormalised spherical harmonics:

An
21+1

Cim(8, 9) = Yim(9, $) (2.136)

Regardless of their minimality, and the elegant quantum-mechanical formula-
tion of QTAIM multipoles, they are endowed of certain attractive properties that

will make them a key part of the research described in this thesis.

One of the features of topological multipoles is that their magnitude increases
rapidly with the rank, with high-rank moments being usually larger than the over-
all molecular moment [87]. This was initially considered a problem; however,
Kosov and Popelier showed that AIM multipoles were adequate to expand the elec-
trostatic potential [177], and that in fact the decay of the electron density favours a

fast convergence [178].

Further studies discovered that, while the convergence of AIM multipoles is in-
ferior to DMA multipoles for small systems, such as water, the former have superior

properties in larger systems [179] such as DNA [180] or crambin [181].

Of comparable importance is the fact that topological multipoles are virtually
basis set independent [182, 183], in contrast to DMA multipoles. This indepen-
dence arises from the real space integration, which is also the source of their main
disadvantages when compared with analytical multipoles: the significant time de-

manded by computation [87], and the existence of small integration errors.


=====[PDF 72 | printed p. 71]=====
2.5. MACHINE LEARNING 71

2.5 Machine learning

The term machine learning was supposedly coined by Arthur L. Samuel in the
fifties, as a paradigm in which computers can learn to perform tasks without being ex-
plicitly programmed [184]. This branch of statistics and computer science, which is
rather undefined due to the enormous advancements in the last few years, com-
prises a large number of techniques, mathematical and computational, sprouted

from different areas.

Aside from recent developments, arising from the so-called deep learning revolu-
tion [16] that uses very specialised neural networks to solve a wide range of prob-
lems, the impact of machine learning in the scientific community is mostly related
to non-parametric statistical regression models. Despite the awe of the name, most
of these approaches are actually quite simple, being generalisations of well-known
statistical models, such as least squares regression or simple interpolation methods.
For example, kriging can be shown to be a generalisation of interpolation by splines
[185].

In this section, we will introduce Gaussian Process regression (also known as
kriging), a very powerful non-parametric regression method that is employed by

the Quantum Chemical Topology Force Field to build its potential.

2.5.1 Gaussian process regression

Gaussian process regression is one of the most powerful techniques in machine
learning. Its origin is usually linked to geostatistics, where it is used for ore predic-
tion [186], although we note that its use can actually be traced back to Laplace. This
technique received special attention in the nineties, when it was used by Sacks et al
to analyse the output of computer simulations [187]. Nowadays, it is mostly used

in Bayesian optimisation [188].

Here, we will follow a modern derivation in the spirit of [189]. In general, given
a set of features x from the system, we want to predict a certain response y (which

we will assume scalar without loss of generality), following certain observations


=====[PDF 73 | printed p. 72]=====
72 CHAPTER 2. LITERATURE REVIEW

{xx}, {ye}:
yi = f (xi) + (xi) (2.137)

Note that we assume that our observations (and our predictions!) can be cor-
rupted by a noise e. Also, this error can be considered to be dependent on the
features (heteroscedastic regression), although we will assume that the variance is

constant (homoscedastic regression) without loss of generality.

The distinguishing feature of Gaussian process regression is that we assume
that the regression function f can be taken as the sample from a Gaussian process:
a stochastic process (i. e. a collection, perhaps infinite, of random variables) in which
every finite subcollection of random variables follow a multivariate normal distri-

bution (the generalisation of a normal distribution to several variables).

In general, this statement might be quite confusing, so it is useful to obtain an
intuitive understanding. Let us take a function f € £7; it is known from functional

analysis that it can be expanded in a complete basis:

F(x) = cee) (2.138)
k=0

If we fix a given complete basis, any possible function in £? can be represented
simply by choosing the right coefficients c;,. It is worth to pay special attention to
these coefficients; in particular, a given set of coefficients can be considered as an

element of C®, the space of all infinite sequences of complex numbers.

{c,} = (cy, C2, ..-) €ce (2.139)

Now, let us assign an univariate normal distribution N’(1,7) to each of these
coefficients. Let us assume that, in the position k, the mean 4; is equal to the coef-
ficient c, in the expansion of a given “true” regression function f, and the standard
deviation 7, which is the same for every k due to our assumption of homoscedas-

ticity, represents the error.


=====[PDF 74 | printed p. 73]=====
2.5. MACHINE LEARNING 73

This is completely equivalent to a probability distribution in a space of func-
tions! In particular, we have a Gaussian process whose mean is the regression func-
tion f, but also all the possible functions resulting from measures corrupted by error

are considered.

This short digression has probably clarified how to define a probability distribu-
tion over a space of functions. It has also offered a glimpse to our objective: obtain
a stochastic process whose mean corresponds to a given objective function, and a
variance that quantifies the expected error (and thus, hopefully, provide a reason-

able error estimate to our predictions).

There is, however, one caveat. Specifying the stochastic process completely re-
quires then an infinite number of coefficients (actually, an infinite number of means,
and an infinite number of standard deviations). This is, in practice, impossible: not
only is it impossible to store all this information in a computer, but the mere recol-
lection of an infinite number of samples is a doomed procedure. We note that, in
case an infinite number of points is available, it is probably more efficient to simply

store the value of the function f at every possible point.

Let us accept the restriction to a finite number of points. In this case, we will
take the regression function to be given from a Gaussian process, which in the finite

case is simply a multivariate normal distribution:

£ = [f (x1), f (x2), f(xw)|’ ~ N(0,K) (2.140)

Here K is the covariance matrix (also called correlation matrix, and typically de-
noted by F in the literature), which models the correlation between the different
points. This matrix plays an extremely important role on the performance of Gaus-
sian process regression; obviously, it contains the information through which we
try to model an infinite problem by means of a finite number of samples. However,

we will only address its form later on.

Continuing with the derivation, it follows from the previous equation that the

dependence of y on f is thus:


=====[PDF 75 | printed p. 74]=====
74 CHAPTER 2. LITERATURE REVIEW

(y|x) = (ys-y, NI" If) ~ N(£,0°T) (2.141)

Let us consider a test input, which we will denote by x*. We want to rearrange
the probability distribution that yields f in a way that produces a response to x*;
and we want to define f using the sets of evaluations S = {x,, y,}. We can do this

by using the sum rule of probability, which in the continuous case is an integral:

P(f*|x*,S) = / P(f*, £|x*, S) dé (2.142)

This last equation can also be interpreted as an average over all possible func-
tional forms that is weighted by the agreement with the observations S. Now, using

the product rule of probability we arrive to:

P(f*|x*,S) = / P(f*|£,x*, S)P(f*|x*, S) df (2.143)

We will now work on each of the terms in this equation until we obtain a simple
expression that allows to perform training and prediction using only methods from
linear algebra that can be easily implemented in a computer. However, it is worth
to stop briefly on equation 2.143, and appreciate why Gaussian processes are so

important.

Although in the general case an infinite-dimensional Gaussian process can rep-
resent any function, there is no reason to assume it is the best model. In fact, they
are inherently bad to model certain processes, with financial data, plagued by sharp

discontinuities, being a good example [190].

However, Gaussian process have a significant advantage that makes them widely
used even when they are not the most appropriate model: they are analytically
tractable. For most of the probability distributions, evaluating integrals such as
equation 2.143 is extremely involved and requires expensive techniques such as
Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC), or severe approximations such as variational
inference [191]. Hereafter, we will assume that a Gaussian model is adequate, as is

probably true for the majority of problems related to the objective of this thesis.


=====[PDF 76 | printed p. 75]=====
2.5. MACHINE LEARNING 75

refer the reader to [192] for a proof. Applying this, we arrive to:

P(f*|£) ~ N(k'K1£, k(x*, x*) — k7C~1k) (2.145)

However, the probability distributions on the right hand side have been defined

In practice, we require the kernel to also fulfil certain notions of similarity. In

this context, it is usual to quote Tobler’s first law of geography [193]:

Everything is related to everything else, but near things are more related
than distant things.

We note that the generality of the kernel functions has allowed to extend the
Gaussian process formalism towards all kind of areas, including text, graphs, chem-

informatic models and even chemical reactions.
