2.5.2 Maximum likelihood estimation

This subsection is concerned with how to train a kriging model. This might sound a
bit strange: in the previous subsection we have described and derived a set of equa-
tions that allow to obtain a predictive model f (x*) from a training set S = {x;, yx}.
There is, however, a problem that we have not addressed yet: given a kernel func-
tional form, such as equation 2.155, how can we estimate the hyperparameters (in this

case, @ and p) that provide a best estimation of the data?

The first idea that might come to mind is selecting a set of points that are not
contained in the training set S, and vary the hyperparameters {6;} in the way that
best recovers the true values of these points. This idea is strong because of several
reasons: it does not introduce any new assumption to the model, and in a certain
way, it allows to correct some of the inadequacies of the model. However, it has
two very strong difficulties: it requires an additional set of training points, and it is,

in general, very expensive computationally [190].

In the world of limited data and finite computational resources, a different ap-
proach is generally required. This passes by introducing a new probabilistic model
to estimate the hyperparameters. The most extended approach is called maximum
likelihood estimation; here, we estimate the probability P(y|x, {6,}), which can be

written as (in logarithmic form, which is more amenable)!!:

lee, 1 N
log P(y|{xx}, {9x}) = L({8}) — 5y'K ty — 5 log|K|— > log2n (2.159)

11Jn the following equations we have assumed a deterministic model in which there is no noise.
The generalisation for 7 # 0 is straightforward.


=====[PDF 80 | printed p. 79]=====
2.5. MACHINE LEARNING 79

Here N is the number of training points in S, and we remark that K is dependent
on the hyperparameters {6;,}. What this expression gives is the probability that we
obtain the known responses y at the known points {x;,} given the hyperparameters
{9,}. This function is called likelihood in the statistical jargon, or log-likelihood when

it is presented in logarithmic form.

If we aim to build a reasonable model, it seems reasonable to choose a set of
hyperparameters that make predicting y;, at x; very likely; in other words to select
the hyperparameters that maximise the likelihood’. In fact, the following theorem
[194] proves that this is a good approach:

Theorem 1 (Maximum likelihood estimation) Let X = (Xj, X2,...) be a collection
of N random variables with a probability distribution f(x; 0) dependent on a parameter 0,

and let 09 be the true parameter for that distribution. Then, under the following conditions:

1. The probability distributions are distinct, i. e. V0, # 0, then f(x;01) A f(x; 02).

2. All probability distributions have a common support (i. e. the set of values of the

random variable with non-zero probability) for all 0,

Then, it holds:
jim Po, [L(80,X) > L(0,X)} =1 VO F A (2.160)
oo

In other words, as the number of random variables increases, the likelihood of the real value

0 is larger than the likelihood of any other possible point.

The proof of this theorem is quite simple, but requires analysing the concept of
convergence in probability for which we have no space here. The interested reader

is referred to [194] for a very clear presentation of the topic.

This result ensures that, if we get an infinite number of samples from the partic-
ular problem we want to model, the likelihood of the true hyperparameter 69 will
be greater than for any other value, i. e., it will be a global maximum. There are,

however, three caveats that are not evident from the above theorem.

l2Note that, since the logarithm is a monotonic function, maximising the log-likelihood is totally
equivalent to maximising the likelihood.


=====[PDF 81 | printed p. 80]=====
80 CHAPTER 2. LITERATURE REVIEW

In the first place, it is obviously impossible to obtain an infinite number of sam-
ples, so it remains to prove whether the maximum likelihood estimation is still valid
for a finite number of measurements. This can be seen easily with a toy example,

that the chemical reader might remember from elementary chemometrics.

Let us imagine that we are sampling from a Gaussian distribution N (1,7), per-
haps by performing complexation titration to measure hardness in water, and that
we are interested in obtaining the parameters p (the “real” value) and ¢ (an estima-
tion of the error). Let us apply the maximum likelihood estimation approach, with
the likelihood being simply a product of Gaussian probabilities:

_Ty 1. -G—")
L(u,c) = IT Jano exp ( 302 (2.161)

By applying logarithms, and equating the derivatives with respect to » and o to

zero, it is easy to arrive to the following formulas:

1 N
MLE = 3 ye x (2.162)
k=1

1 N
OMLE = N Ye (xe — ete) (2.163)
k=l

The first formula looks exactly like the universally known definition of the arith-
metic mean, but the variance does not quite look like what is written in the text-
books. In fact, it can be proved that equation 2.163 is a biased estimator, i. e. an

estimator whose expected value is not the quantity that it is supposed to estimate:

E [ote] = oe (2.164)

It is clear that, in the limit N — oo, the maximum likelihood estimation of the
variance equals the right value (as ensured by the theorem vide supra); however, for
small N, the estimation can be significantly distinct. In practice, this is solved by
introducing Bessel’s correction, that yields the appropriate expected value and is the

well-known formula for the sample variance:


=====[PDF 82 | printed p. 81]=====
2.5. MACHINE LEARNING 81

1 N

OMLE -N-1 wes —- UMLE)” (2.165)
k=1

However, we have shown a trivial counterexample that maximum likelihood
estimation is not necessarily correct for a finite number of datapoints. Solutions like
Bessel’s correction are in general not that trivial, and usually empirical knowledge

determines the validity of MLE.

A second caveat is perhaps more subtle. The above mentioned theorem ensures
that MLE can yield the true parameter for the distribution f(x; 6), but nothing has
been said about that distribution being correct. If one tries to model a parabola of
points with a linear model, although the estimated parameters will be the “true”
values for the equation of the line, the model will not fit the data at all.

In the case of kriging, this mostly refers to the selection of an adequate model of
the covariance. If the selected kernel does not adequately reflect the correlation, the

parameters obtained by maximum likelihood estimation will be quite poor.

The third caveat is of technical nature: maximum likelihood estimation only
works for the global maximum of the likelihood, and in general this cannot be done
analytically. Moreover, from equation 2.159 it follows that the inverse of the co-
variance matrix K has to be computed; this requires a Cholesky decomposition that

scales as O(N?), which can result in enormous computational expenses.

The field of global optimisation is a rich area by itself. In the following subsec-
tion we discuss some of the algorithms that can be employed for maximum likeli-

hood estimation.

We note that there are other methods beyond MLE to obtain the hyperparam-
eters of a kriging model [190], mostly of Bayesian nature, although those require
usually operations with prohibitive computational expenses, such as the evalua-

tion of very high-dimensional integrals by Markov Chain Monte Carlo algorithms.


=====[PDF 83 | printed p. 82]=====
82 CHAPTER 2. LITERATURE REVIEW

2.5.3 Global optimisation

The field of global optimisation is an active branch of applied mathematics that is
concerned with methods to obtain the global maximum or minimum in a particular
problem. This field is very rich and comprises many methods; here we will only
consider the approaches that are widely used in Gaussian process regression, either

by the community or within the Popelier group.

We note that algorithms for maximising a function can be used identically to
minimise a function, simply by the transformation f’ = —f. Here we will adopt

the convention of minimisation.

The simplest algorithm to find a minimum in a function is the algorithm known
as gradient descent. This algorithm is based in the mathematical fact that the gradi-
ent of the function at a given point is directed towards the direction of maximum
growth of that function. Thus a simple procedure passes by iteratively subtracting

the gradient until a minimum is reached:

Xeu1 = Xe — YV F(x) (2.166)

Here 7¥ is the length of the step, that in most modern implementations is cho-
sen at each step in an adaptive fashion; one of the most extended is the Broyden-
Fletcher-Goldfarb-Shanno (BFGS) algorithm. There are also a number of sophisti-
cated variations that employ previous steps to modify the gradient or the +.

This method is well-known in computational chemistry, since it is used routinely
to obtain equilibrium geometries. However, it is affected by an obvious problem:
usually, it cannot reach the global minimum, since the gradient path will collapse

in the closest minimum, that acts as an attractor in the dynamical systems sense.

An alternative passes by simply performing several gradient descent optimisa-
tions initialised at a set of random points in space (this is also known, in the field
of potential energy surfaces, as the basin-hopping algorithm [195]). This is favoured
by the fact that gradient descent runs are generally very cheap, although several

runs might be necessary to reach the global minimum. We note that this approach


=====[PDF 84 | printed p. 83]=====
2.5. MACHINE LEARNING 83

is standard in the practical totality of available packages.

However, our group has explored more sophisticated algorithms to obtain the
global minimum, since the number and size of models that have to be trained rou-
tinely is so large that the number of runs required to ensure that the global mini-
mum has been reached is too expensive computationally. We describe here the Par-
ticle Swarm Optimisation (PSO) method, that is the standard in the FEREBUS code
used in our group. Another algorithm, Differential Evolution (DE) is not presented
by lack of space, and can be found in [196].

The PSO algorithm has a very interesting story; it was initially proposed as a
model for collective behaviour of animals, when the researchers discovered that it

actually had a natural tendency to find the global minimum of a function [197].

In the PSO, the function is explored by a swarm of particles, that move freely
around the landscape while maintaining a certain collective behaviour. Let us de-
note by x the position of a given particle in the hyperparameter space; at a time

t + 1, it will be:

xi(f +1) = x;(t) + vi(t+ 1) (2.167)

Here v; is the displacement of the particle between ft — 1 and t, which is known
as velocity in the jargon. The formulation of this increment captures all the power of

the PSO algorithm:

vi(t + 1) = wv;(t) +0, 1] : (b;(t) - x;(t)) + e240, 1] : (g(t) _ x;(t)) (2.168)
“HY wan

momentum cognitive learning social learning

Here, w is called the inertia of the particle, c; is the cognitive learning factor, c2 is
the social learning factor, b;(t) is the position with minimum value that the particle
has visited before, and g(t) is the equivalent value for all the particles. Finally,

U0, 1] is a sample from the uniform distribution in the interval [0, 1).

The first term in equation 2.168 represents the momentum of the particle: how


=====[PDF 85 | printed p. 84]=====
84 CHAPTER 2. LITERATURE REVIEW

much the previous velocity influences the current one. The consecutive two terms
represent how the particle is attracted by its best position, and by the best position
that the swarm has visited. In this way, the particle is allowed to explore the land-
scape of the function (global exploration), while carefully checking the surround-

ings of previously found minima (local exploitation).

Of course, to ensure that the algorithm works properly, it is necessary to find
appropriate parameters for w, cj and cz. Fortunately, this algorithm has been well

studied, and fine-tuned parameters are available [198].

2.6 The Quantum Chemical Topology Force Field
2.6.1 Previous work

The Popelier work has been working for more than a decade in the development of
a novel polarisable force field that is able to correctly describe electrostatic interac-
tions by means of conformationally-dependent multipoles. This is done by employ-
ing Gaussian process regression to predict topological multipoles computed from
the Quantum Theory of Atoms in Molecules. We will briefly present a historical re-
view of the most important feats in this development; a comprehensive description

of the formulation is given in Chapter 3.

The origins of this idea can be traced back to the early research in QTAIM multi-
poles described in Section 2.4.4, especially regarding the good mathematical prop-
erties of these objects. Given the fast convergence to the quantum electrostatic po-
tential, Liem and Popelier proposed a force field based on rigid molecules endowed
with static, high-order multipoles to obtain accurate electrostatics. This potential
was tested almost 15 years ago for hydrogen fluoride [199] and water [200], with
very promising results. In particular, in the former they found the spontaneous for-
mation of chains of hydrogen-bonded monomers, as well as very good agreement

of calculated bulk properties with experiment; similar results were found for water.

Grounded on this work, the group started the development of a flexible polaris-
able force field based in conformationally-dependent topological multipoles, which

was initially named QCTFF (Quantum Chemical Topology Force Field), although


=====[PDF 86 | printed p. 85]=====
2.6. THE QUANTUM CHEMICAL TOPOLOGY FORCE FIELD 85

was later renamed as FFLUX. Most of the work was dedicated to find a way to pre-
dict conformationally-dependent multipoles avoiding a self-consistent procedure
(which is employed in inducible dipole force fields and is a main cause of the high
computational cost). The key idea was to employ machine learning to fit the per-

manent multipoles of a molecule to its conformational degrees of freedom.

Initial studies assessed the performance of multilayer perceptrons, a type of neu-
ral networks, for predicting multipolar moments for hydrogen fluoride [201], water
[202, 203] and shortly after glycine and N-methylacetamide [19]. However, after a
comparative study of several machine learning methods, including multilayer per-
ceptrons, radial basis function neural networks and kriging [204], it was decided
that the latter was the most adequate. Subsequent work showed that kriging out-
performed artificial neural networks in water [205], ethanol [206], alanine [207],
histidine [208] and serine [209].

Later on, after testing the multipole model in rigid body simulations of imida-
zole [210], and the electrostatic potential of crambin [181], the current structure of
FFLUX was developed in a paper by Mills and Popelier including the explicit for-
mulas for the electrostatic forces [206].

The last years have seen several improvements in the basics of the method, that
include the improvement of the sampling method [211], progresses in the featuriza-
tion [212] and the development of a highly parallelized kriging engine [196, 213].
The prediction of multipoles has also been tested in several systems of larger size,
including cholesterol [214], carbohydrates [215], aminoacids [216] and small pep-
tides [217].

Finally, during last year, the work from Popelier group has been applied to the
geometry optimisation of a single water molecule [218] and peptide-capped glycine
[219], which sets a start for the upscaling of the project.


=====[PDF 87 | printed p. 86]=====
Chapter 3

The Quantum Chemical Topology
Force Field

N the previous chapter we presented an extended literature review of the
state-of-the-art in molecular simulation, with an emphasis on intermolec-
ular interactions in the context of force fields. We also provided a brief

historical review of the major feats in the development of the Quantum Chemical
Topology Force Field (QCTFF, recently renamed as FFLUX), an ambitious research
theme within our group during the last decade.

The FFLUX project has pioneered the application of machine learning to substi-
tute old force field architectures with non-parametric regression, an idea that has
had a significant impact in the literature recently [15]. However, despite the time
invested in this project (around a decade since the first developments in variable
multipoles [204]), the maximum feats have been the optimisation of water [218]
and capped glycine [219], which seem in significant delay with respect to the latest
advancements in the field [17, 18].

There are several factors that have hindered the progress of FFLUX in the path
of becoming an useful force field. One of them is the attempt to test the kriging
methodology deeply — and thus several publications from the group [205, 206, 207,
208, 209] described repeatedly the same process of training a kriging model in dif-
ferent related molecules. Another feat is the lengthy development of FEREBUS

86


=====[PDF 88 | printed p. 87]=====
3.1. ARCHITECTURE OF FFLUX 87

[213], a highly parallelized kriging engine that was conceived after the necessity
to produce accurate atomistic kriging models in a reasonable amount of time (al-
though it ignored already existing kriging engines developed by domain experts
[220, 221, 222, 223, 224]).

After a decade of work, it seems that the simple examples previously employed
are starting to meet the demands of actual simulations. In this context, it is neces-
sary to wonder: what are the main challenges in the development of FFLUX, and
how should they be tackled? The present chapter is dedicated to analyse this ques-
tion.

This chapter is structured as follows. First, in Section 3.1 we describe the archi-
tecture of FFLUX, including a description of the potentials and the featurization.
We also provide a complete explicit derivation of the forces in Section 3.2, and then
a description of the training process in Section 3.3. After exposing the main back-
ground, section 3.4 provides an analysis of some of the challenges in FFLUX. Finally,
in section 3.5 we gather some conclusions and prepare for further developments in

Chapters 4 and 5.

3.1 Architecture of FFLUX

3.1.1 The potential: knowledgeable quantum atoms

The Quantum Chemical Topology Force Field breaches with traditional force field
expressions (harmonic potentials, torsion terms, etc.) by modelling a system in
terms of knowledgeable quantum atoms [20], a set of atomistic kriging models that
try to mimic the properties of quantum atoms (atomic energies and multipolar mo-
ments) by interpolation of previously seen examples. In this way, instead of ’clas-
sicalising” interactions by means of approximate formulas, the system is modelled

fully from a quantum mechanical perspective using non-parametric regression.

The potential has the following form:

EELux = E1ga + Electrostatic + Edispersion (3.1)


=====[PDF 89 | printed p. 88]=====
88 CHAPTER 3. THE QUANTUM CHEMICAL TOPOLOGY FORCE FIELD

Figure 3.1: Scheme of interactions in the FFLUX force field. The valence potential
is obtained fully from a machine-learned Ejgq potential. Dispersion is modelled
by a Lennard-Jones potential, and electrostatics include multipole moments up to

hexadecapole.

This potential can be better understood in the context of an example simulation,
such as the water cluster depicted in Figure 3.1. As in classical molecular mechan-
ics, there will be intramolecular and intermolecular interactions. In the first place,
every individual water molecule contributes an energy Ejga({Ra}), where {R,}
indicates that it is dependent on the total molecular configuration, and as such this

potential is reminiscent of the valence, intramolecular terms in classical force fields.

The usage of Ejgq has a number of advantages. In the first place, it makes a
direct connection with quantum mechanics, as the IQA self-energy rigorously parti-
tions the total energy. In the second place, given the non-parametric nature of atom-
istic kriging models, it might be feasible! in the following years to study processes
where bond breaking and forming is involved, competing with the few existing re-
active force fields [225]. Overall, it allows to recover the total quantum-mechanical

energy for a single molecule.

The interaction between molecules is given by two components: dispersion and

1We note, however, that very significant changes should be made to the current formulation.
For example, it is not clear how the features of a molecule, described in Section 3.1.2, would change
upon dissociation.


=====[PDF 90 | printed p. 89]=====
3.1. ARCHITECTURE OF FFLUX 89

multipolar electrostatics. The dispersion is currently based on the classical 12-6
Lennard-Jones potential, although there is ongoing work to implement a more ad-
vanced potential based on variable-input kriging [226] trained on topologically par-
titioned dispersion energies [227]. On the other hand, the electrostatics is the key

part of the FFLUX force field, and can be summarised in the following equation:

co ls oo lz
EG? srostatic = >» >» yi - QP maa ({Ra}) Qi ms ({Ra}) Teme (3.2)

14=0 m4g=—l, lgp=0 mg=—Ip

Here, Qf malt R,}) is the prediction of a kriging model that captures a given
multipolar moment in terms of the atomic configuration of the molecule. This is
necessary since atomic multipoles can be highly dependent on the conformation
[228]. Additionally, the good convergence [177, 179, 229] and transferability [230]
of QTAIM multipoles advocate for its usability in a force field. The charge distri-
butions of both atoms are described by multipole moments up to hexadecapole,

necessary to accurately converge to the molecular electrostatic potential.

Multipolar electrostatics can seem quite obscure for those not familiar with its
terminology. In Appendix C we provide a complete derivation of multipolar elec-
trostatics employing the Cartesian tensor formalism, as well as the formulae for the
different interactions and interaction tensors up to quadrupole. The conclusion of
this treatment, as simple as it is, must be borne in mind: multipole moments are
nothing more than the coefficients in a Taylor expansion”, and if the latter converges, it

is clear that adding more terms improves the description of the potential.

We note that a significant assumption in the FFLUX scaffold is that charge trans-
fer can be adequately modelled by the changes induced in the geometry. This as-

sumption will be explored in more detail vide infra.

3.1.2. The atomic local frame

Multipole moments of order greater than zero are tensor magnitudes, which are de-

pendent on the system of coordinates in which they are defined. This frame of

The spherical tensor formulation is not much different, but has the advantage that implicitly
accounts for the symmetries in the formulae, simplifying the treatment significantly.


=====[PDF 91 | printed p. 90]=====
90 CHAPTER 3. THE QUANTUM CHEMICAL TOPOLOGY FORCE FIELD

reference must be specified in order to obtain sensible multipoles; it is also impor-
tant in the process of multipole rotation, described in Appendix C.5. In this section
we will present the Atomic Local Frame (ALF), which is used in FFLUX, not only to
define the multipoles, but also the features. We report the definition given by [231]
and corrected by [219, 232].

As will be discussed in Section 3.2, the formulae for the forces in FFLUX depend
on the assumption that the kriging model uses the positions of the molecule as fea-
tures. One could, in principle, employ the coordinates of the molecule in a global
Cartesian frame, but these contain six superfluous degrees of freedom (three of po-
sition and three of rotation) that are not required, and that are a source of errors and
inaccuracy during training. The power of the ALF resides in its ability to eliminate

these degrees of freedom.

As its name implies, the ALF is defined per atom. The coordinates of every other

atom in the molecule are given by:

chosen so that both QO, and Ox, are contained in the xy plane.

the forces:

3.3 The training of kriging models

The last two sections have provided a comprehensive treatment of the archi-
tecture and potentials employed in the Quantum Chemical Topology Force Field
(FFLUX). We shall now complete this description by addressing the parametrization

process of the force field: how are the atomistic kriging models trained.

The details of the training are best specified by presenting the actual parametriza-
tion pipeline. This relies in GAIA, a long Perl script that has been developed in the
Popelier group during the past few years [234, 217]. GAIA automates most of the
operations, interacting with several commercial and in-house codes, and providing

checks to ensure that every step is performed correctly.

A basic scheme is depicted in Figure 3.3. The process starts by obtaining a set of
geometries through a normal mode distortion procedure, described in [211] and its
Supplementary Material, and performed with the in-house code TYCHE. The input
for this program can be considered as the input for GAIA: an optimised, or set of

optimised geometries, known as seeds, and the corresponding Hessians (which are


=====[PDF 100 | printed p. 99]=====
3.3. THE TRAINING OF KRIGING MODELS 99

Geometry distortion || TYCHE

I

ab initio calculations | GAUSSIAN 09 >

! 5
<< ©
=z IQA integration I AIMAII —

oO rs}

] ae)

Multipole rotation I NORMART Lae)

|

Training = FEREBUS

Figure 3.3: Scheme of the training pipeline. The training procedure is handled by
the GAIA Perl script, whose stages are denoted by orange boxes. Blue boxes repre-

sent in-house programs, while green boxes denote commercial software.

obtained from GAUSSIAN 09 [235] using the keyword punch=derivatives).

TYCHE outputs a set of distorted geometries around the input configurations,
which are checked and shuffled by GAIA. Then GAUSSIAN 09 is employed to per-
form ab initio calculations at the specified level of theory. The input files contain the
output=wfn keyword, which ensures that the molecular orbitals (or natural orbitals

for correlated wavefunctions) are printed in a .wfn file.

These orbitals constitute the input for the AIMAII code [236], which constructs
the density and performs the partition of real space in atomic basins. These are in
turn integrated to yield the atomic multipoles (in the spherical tensor formulation),
and the Ejga values. These quantities (along with some others, such as atomic

kinetic energies) are parsed and extracted by GAIA.

The multipoles as computed by AIMAII are defined in the global frame, and
must be rotated to the ALF before the kriging models can be trained. This is done
by the in-house code NORMART, which handles all the spherical harmonic algebra

and outputs plain-text files containing the features and responses for the training


=====[PDF 101 | printed p. 100]=====
100 CHAPTER 3. THE QUANTUM CHEMICAL TOPOLOGY FORCE FIELD

set.

Finally, the training sets are passed to FEREBUS [213], a highly-parallelized
in-house kriging engine, that performs the actual training, resembling the proce-
dure described in 2.5.2. The output from FEREBUS can be provided directly to
DL_FFLUX, the implementation of FFLUX in the DL-POLY 4 code [237, 232].

3.4 Accritical appraisal

Every model of physical reality is subject to errors, and understanding when and
why these errors are important is perhaps as crucial as employing them correctly.
This might be clarified with a simple example. Hartree-Fock theory is very success-
ful at predicting geometries and thermochemical properties of organic compounds;
however, employing this same method to metallic complexes will lead to disas-
trous conclusions. This does not imply that the theory itself is wrong: it means that
metallic complexes are beyond its range of applicability. Similarly, understanding
the reasons behind the failure, in this case the existence of static correlation, allows

to design more powerful methods that can cope with new problems.

Here, after thoroughly presenting the formulation of FFLUX, we will try to dis-
cuss some of its weaknesses. In this context, these shall not be understood as a
criticism to the force field, but rather an understanding of its limitations that allows
to consider its development and applications carefully. As an example, the curse of
dimensionality presented in Section 3.4.3 implies that handling proteins will only
be possible after introducing severe approximations in the models. However, this
same difficulty does not apply to water molecules, and perhaps FFLUX should be
focused in producing a cheap and accurate model of solvation, a development long

awaited by the quantum chemical community [238].

The difficulties presented here will be based in physical and mathematical rea-
sonings, and will also be exemplified with some simple numerical verifications. It is
not the main objective of this thesis, or of this chapter, to treat these problems in de-
tail, and so the discussion will be kept as concise and simple as possible. Similarly,
the examples are brief and self-contained, although it will become evident that they

are representative of the respective phenomena, rather than anecdotal findings.


=====[PDF 102 | printed p. 101]=====
3.4. A CRITICAL APPRAISAL 101

The discussions in this section do not necessarily agree with the vision held

currently by the Popelier group, and represent the observations of the thesis’ author.

3.4.1 The challenge of polarisation

We will first assess the lack of polarisation in FFLUX, which is a problem in its
physical description of intermolecular interactions, rather than a technical prob-
lem related to the details of the parametrization. This arises from one of the major
assumptions of the force field: that the charge distribution depends only on the
molecular geometry, and that charge transfer effects are governed only by confor-

mational changes.

Turning to the electrostatic interaction described in equation 3.2, one might eas-
ily observe that the charges (and higher-order multipoles) on every atomic site are
obtained from the kriging models, which depend on the molecular geometry alone.
There is, in principle, no direct influence of the environment: two molecules in com-
pletely different environments will show the same charges, as long as both share
the same geometry. In other words, the environment affects the molecular charge

distribution only by distorting the geometry.

This assumption means that, although FFLUX has been presented as a polaris-
able force field, in rigour it can only be categorised as a multipolar force field, since
the electrostatics do not respond directly to the changes in the environment. In
other words, the polarisation is intramolecular rather than intermolecular, in contrast
to the polarisable force fields presented in Section 2.3, where both forms are present.
For example, in fluctuating charge force fields, the intermolecular response is repre-
sented by a charge redistribution until the electronegativity constraints are fulfilled.
Similarly, in inducible dipole force fields, this is accounted explicitly employing
Applequist model. In both cases, two molecules with the same geometry will have
different charge distributions if their environments differ.

Neglecting intermolecular polarisation could in principle be an important asset,
especially in terms of efficiency, since it is known that this step consumes a signifi-

cant amount of computer time (for example, in the AMOEBA force field it amounts


=====[PDF 103 | printed p. 102]=====
102 CHAPTER 3. THE QUANTUM CHEMICAL TOPOLOGY FORCE FIELD

to about 50% of the cost [130]). However, to date no study has been performed to

analyse whether this approximation is reasonable or not.

It is not difficult to come up with simple examples that challenge this interpre-
tation. For example, in a protein, the environment of a residue will be considerably
different depending on whether it is placed in the hydrophobic core of the pep-
tide, or in the surface, exposed to the solvent [103, 104]. Similarly, it is likely that
the constraint imposed by the rest of the residues limits the flexibility of the sin-
gle aminoacid, affecting the intramolecular polarisation. The approach in FFLUX
might find difficult to model these systems, especially in comparison with success-

ful formulations such as AMOEBA that include polarisation explicitly.

We propose the following simple experiment, as an initial test of the validity
of the approximation. We will select a system of several molecules, and compare
the electrostatic energy as calculated with its permanent atomic multipoles only (i.
e. the multipoles computed for each of its constituent molecules, in vacuum), and
with the real multipoles, that include the polarisation components (i. e. the atomic
multipoles computed for each atom in the total system). This emulates the process
that occurs during a simulation, where the atomic multipoles of each molecule are
obtained by interpolation of in vacuo values, and avoids other possible sources of

error.

Given the importance of polarisation in water, and the ubiquity of this solvent in
biological systems, we will employ as test system a cluster of five water molecules.
To obtain several conformations, we built an arbitrary system using Avogadro [239],
pre-optimised it using MMFF94 [240], and ran 5 ps BP86/Def2SVP BOMD simula-
tions at 600 K employing ORCA [241] and the Berendsen thermostat. Obviating
equilibration, five snapshots were sampled randomly, and their geometries were
optimised at the RI-MP2/cc-pVTZ level of theory. Note that the only objective of
the BOMD simulation is to distort the system and obtain different conformations,
so the difference in methods and basis sets is unimportant. Finally, single point
MP2/cc-pVTZ calculations on the optimal cluster geometries were performed with
GAUSSIAN 09 [235].

The in vacuo atomic multipoles up to quadrupole were calculated employing


=====[PDF 104 | printed p. 103]=====

We show the calculated energies in Table 3.1, where E
ergy calculated with the multipoles of isolated molecules (and thus unpolarised),
and Egiec is the electrostatic energy calculated with the cluster multipoles (which
therefore includes polarisation). It is clear that there is an error of approximately
400 kcal mol“! by neglecting polarisation; we also observed that the charges in the

cluster are changed by an average of 0.10+0.05 e.

A large percentage of this omission will be compensated by error cancellation
when computing energy differences, although certainly not all. For example, the
energy difference between the first two snapshots in Table 3.1 (neglecting disper-
sion and higher-order multipoles, that are not included) is about 8 kcal mol~! when
polarisation is accounted for, while the purely electrostatic result is about 8 kcal
mol! with opposite sign. In other words: the intermolecular interactions in FFLUX
cannot even rank conformers by stability, even when the energetic difference is sig-

nificant.

Obviating this last point, the total energy difference is about 16 kcal mol™!,

which is still non-negligible. There are many applications where differences of this

3The code employed to compute the electrostatics has been validated against DL_POLY 4 and
several manual calculations.


=====[PDF 105 | printed p. 104]=====
104 CHAPTER 3. THE QUANTUM CHEMICAL TOPOLOGY FORCE FIELD

magnitude can be very detrimental for the performance, including scoring func-
tions, which is one of the main objectives of FFLUX. It must be taken into account
that predicting multipolar moments with FFLUX is very computationally expen-
sive: it requires evaluating hundreds, and often thousands of exponentials. If this
process still yields multipoles that produce such inaccuracies, it is likely that the

formulation of FFLUX will need readjustments.

Overall, these results suggest that intermolecular polarisation is indeed impor-
tant in the description of many phenomena. Future work should test the impor-
tance of polarisation in other systems, and attempt to introduce possible correc-
tions, perhaps the introduction of a self-consistent polarisation process such as the
one presented in Section 2.3.4. Other problems, such as whether multipoles com-
puted from DFT (which are known to be a good approximation in equilibrium, but
not beyond [242]) can accurately account for intramolecular polarisation effects in

charge-transfer situations, should also be considered.

3.4.2 The extrapolation collapse

The other two challenges that we will assess are not related to an improper physi-
cal description, but rather to the inconveniences of the mathematical methods em-
ployed in FFLUX. Unfortunately, the challenge assessed in this section, which we
have named extrapolation collapse after encountering it several times, will actually
lead to an unphysical behaviour that can put in danger the conclusions of the sim-

ulations

The extrapolation collapse is a phenomenon occurring whenever a prediction
is done outside the training range. In general, extrapolation beyond the domain of
applicability of any model is a dangerous move. Attempting to apply a linear model
to non-linear physical phenomena, or employing statistical conclusions outside the
population where they were recorded, is doomed to fail. However, in the case of
FFLUx, the subtleties of Gaussian process regression make extrapolation especially

problematic.

We will explain this phenomenon from a mathematical point of view. Let us


=====[PDF 106 | printed p. 105]=====
3.4. A CRITICAL APPRAISAL 105

return to the prediction equations:

Neraining Nfeatures 2
y(f*) = p+ w; exp |- au A
u » my) (3.43)

=wt+rR! (y—y1)

Here py is the mean of the Gaussian process, R is the correlation or covariance
matrix, y is the vector containing the responses of the training set, and r is the
correlation vector, that includes the correlation of every point in the training set
with the input f*. Similarly, 6; represents the hyperparameter @ on the jth feature,
and w; represents the weight of the ith training point, which is obtained as the ith

component in the vector R~!(y — y/1).

It is easy to observe that, in the last equation, the only term that depends on the
input f* is the correlation vector r (note that the weights w; and the hyperparame-
ters 6; are all fixed after the model has been trained and do not change at all when

f* changes), whose components we will write explicitly as:

Nfeatures .
1; = exp |- yu Of; - AP (3.44)
J

Let us consider a simple example: that we are trying to predict the Ejgq energy
of the oxygen atom in water. The features are simple: the first two will be the
distances between the oxygen and each hydrogen atom, and the third will be the
HOH angle. Now let us assume that we are trying to compute the dissociation
curve of water by stretching the first O-H bond while keeping the other bond and
the HOH angle constant. Let us also consider that the dissociation curve continues

after the features leave the training set.

As the O-H distance increases, the difference between f; and the correspond-
ing feature in any of the training examples will be greater: the term | ff - fi ? in
equation 3.44 will become larger and larger, and therefore the value of the corre-
lation vector will drop to zero, since the exponential of a large negative number

approaches zero very rapidly. Note that we have implicitly assumed the Gaussian


=====[PDF 107 | printed p. 106]=====
106 CHAPTER 3. THE QUANTUM CHEMICAL TOPOLOGY FORCE FIELD

kernel, although in practice our conclusions will be valid for any sensible kernel,
where closer points will always be more correlated than widely separated observa-

tions.

Now, if the correlation vector approaches zero, the second term in equation 3.43

is eventually negligible, and we arrive at:

y(f") =p for f* very different from any training point (3.45)

In this way, as ||r|| — 0, the prediction tends to yz, which is a constant value,
and thus the derivatives will be null. Therefore, if a given molecule has not been
sampled properly, optimisation becomes impossible. This problem is similar to the
catastrophic lengthening of bonds that is observed in classical force fields, when

anharmonic corrections that include terms up to cubic are used [63].

In practice, we observe that the extrapolation collapse occurs rather soon. An
example is displayed in Figure 3.4 for the dissociation curve of water employing
a state-of-the-art, well-tested model. We observe that the potential is very realistic
around the minimum, where the sampling points concentrate, but soon the O-H
distance is longer than any example in the training set and the potential becomes

flat.

The extrapolation collapse threatens the application of FFLUX beyond toy prob-
lems: for even moderate systems (due to the curse of dimensionality analysed in next
section), for example ethanol or N-methylacetamide, optimisations easily get stuck
in flat surfaces, yielding unreasonable results. The problem is even more acute

when attempting finite-temperature simulations, as we will show.

Employing the same model displayed in Figure 3.4, we implemented the Metropolis-
Hastings algorithm’ and ran a simple Monte Carlo simulation of a single water
molecule in vacuum, where the internal degrees of freedom (two bond lengths and
one angle) were distorted to ensure a 50% acceptance ratio. The evolution of the en-

ergy along the first 2500 simulation steps is shown in Figure 3.5. After 1000 steps,

4The Ejga potential was validated against DL_FFLUX, reproducing the results in [218] and [219],
and the Metropolis-Hastings implementation was validated against the PyMC3 code [243].

Figure 3.4: Dissociation curve of water as computed by FFLUX. The model con-

and computed at the B3LYP/aug-cc-pVTZ level of theory. The flat potential beyond

the training set is a clear example of the extrapolation collapse.

the energy gets stuck in a constant value, and the water molecule get distorted to

absolutely unphysical structures, such as the shown in the left side of Figure 3.6.

Eventually, the simulation ends up climbing up the potential energy barrier of
dissociation; then, since the potential is asymptotically flat as the features leave the
training set, the unphysical distortions carry a AE ~ 0, and are always accepted.
After enough time, the distortion is so strong that the system is far from any physi-

cally sensible behaviour.

We note that it is also possible to find the opposite behaviour: molecules trapped
in a flat potential because the hydrogen atoms are too close to the oxygen. This is
more rare, since the barrier is slightly higher, and there is much more feature space
after dissociation than near the collapse with the oxygen atom, but we have been

able to find it after a handful of successive runs with the same starting geometry.

There is no simple way to solve this problem. A simple solution passes by mak-
ing p a function dependent on the features, perhaps in the form of a classical bond
potential. This prevents the extrapolation collapse, but also cancels most of the

benefits of the non-parametric potential, including the flexibility of the potential

Figure 3.5: Evolution of the energy along a single water Monte Carlo simulation.

of -76.4166 hartree, and does not change independently of the distortion. This is a

Figure 3.6: First (left) and last (right) snapshots of a 2500 step single water Monte

the molecule outside of the training region, where the potential is flat and unphys-

ical behaviour arises.


=====[PDF 110 | printed p. 109]=====
3.4. A CRITICAL APPRAISAL 109

and the promise of being able to handle reactions in the future.

Another possibility would be extended sampling: providing a sampling so ex-
tensive that the system will never leave the training set in a reasonable temperature
simulation. However, this brings up several problems. In the first place, this im-
plies intensive computational expenses, both during the training phase and during
simulation. Additionally, bond dissociation is known to be significantly difficult to
model due to multiconfigurational issues, and the methods to tackle this problem
are usually expensive. Finally, a consequence of the curse of dimensionality dis-
cussed vide infra is that the number of sampling points grows exponentially with
sample size, and thus extended sampling is generally impractical and often impos-
sible.

There is no obvious solution, and the extrapolation collapse threatens to be-
come catastrophic, as it is made evident in unpublished results for moderately-
sized molecules. Future work should aim to develop solutions that minimise the

effect on the force field accuracy.

3.4.3 The curse of dimensionality

In this final section, we will discuss the influence of a well-known problem in statis-
tics and machine learning, the curse of dimensionality, and its implications for the
Quantum Chemical Topology Force Field. This is a problem related to the complex-
ity of the mathematical models involved in the predictions in FFLUX.

The curse of dimensionality can be understood intuitively by analysing the fol-
lowing example. Let us consider that we are trying to numerically evaluate the

multidimensional integral of a certain function f (x):

/ a I F(x) dx (3.46)

The actual domain of integration is unimportant: we will simply assume that
it is a regular n-hypercube. Since the result is trivially extensible to any kind of

quadrature, we will also assume that we are employing a regular evenly-spaced


=====[PDF 111 | printed p. 110]=====
110 CHAPTER 3. THE QUANTUM CHEMICAL TOPOLOGY FORCE FIELD

gl9 9?=81 9°=729
Figure 3.7: A pictorial description of the curse of dimensionality. As the dimension-

ality of the system increases, the number of points required to cover it completely

grows exponentially.

grid to compute the integral. The key question is: how does the size of the grid
evolves as the dimensionality of the integration domain grows? As shown graph-
ically in Figure 3.7, the answer is quite disappointing: this growth is exponential,
and soon becomes enormous. For the example shown, evaluating a six-dimensional
integral would require 9° = 531,441 points, five orders of magnitude above the

one-dimensional integral.

Building a kriging model is in a sense not very different from numerically evalu-
ating an integral: in both cases, we are covering a N-dimensional space with obser-
vations. The only difference is that, instead of employing the weighted sum, we are
interpolating the closest points to produce a prediction. In the same way that nu-
merically evaluating a 50D integral seems barely reasonable, there are also several

limitations to the kriging models that can be produced.

The curse of dimensionality and its consequences are well-known in statistics
and machine learning [244]. We will not provide a complete mathematical discus-
sion of its consequences and computational complexity, which are well beyond the
scope of this thesis. We will simply show how even state-of-the-art models of very
large size are unable to produce reasonable predictions. The experiment shown
here is particularly simple: we will employ the latest published model, capped
glycine [219], and show that it produces absolutely unreasonable responses in per-

fectly reasonable glycine conformations.

5Note that this would require 95° = 5.154 - 10*” points with the quadrature in Figure 3.7.


=====[PDF 112 | printed p. 111]=====
3.4. A CRITICAL APPRAISAL 111

B3LYP/apc-1

FFLUX

500

4. 300

100

Figure 3.8: Distribution of energies in glycine as predicted by B3LYP/apc-1 and
FFLUX. The energies are reported in kcal mol~! and as deviation from the mean.
Note that, although both distributions are superimposed, their means differ by a
systematic error of approximately 600 kcal mol~!. Moreover, the minima (reduced

green examples in the left side of the plot) cannot be ranked.

We first built a list of 200 conformers employing RDKit [245], representing capped
glycine as the SMILES string [246] CC (=0)NC (=0) CNC. These conformations were re-
laxed employing MMFF94 [240] and after a RMSD pruning procedure to discard
geometries that differ by less than 1.0 A from the optimum in [219], the 10 lowest-

energy configurations were selected.

Each of the conformers was then optimised at the B3LYP/apc-1 level of theory
employing ORCA [241] and the RIJCOSX approximation, and the same software
and conditions were employed in 200 fs BOMD simulations at 600 K employing
the Berendsen thermostat. Since the only objective is to distort the conformers, no
equilibration was employed. Finally, the total energies were recalculated employ-
ing GAUSSIAN 09 [235] and the B3LYP/apc-1 level of theory, for consistency with
the original results [219].

This allows to build a test set of 2,000 examples that accurately represents glycine


=====[PDF 113 | printed p. 112]=====
112 CHAPTER 3. THE QUANTUM CHEMICAL TOPOLOGY FORCE FIELD

conformations that could be found during a finite-temperature simulation; we note
that the energy of these geometries is contained in a 4 kcal mol~! range, and thus
accessible to reasonable temperatures. We then employ the glycine model in [219],
containing ~ 0.5 GB of examples, and predict the energies of these configurations in
vacuum. Since the test set explores the conformational space of capped glycine, the
errors allow to assess how much of this space is actually modelled by the kriging
models.

At first sight, the results show significant disagreement, with an average error
of 600 kcal mol~!. The standard deviation is 4 kcal mol~!, and a rapid comparison
shows that the majority of the predictions are contained in a range of 5 kcal mol!
at both sides of the mean of the Gaussian process, 4 = —455.502467 a. u., almost 1
hartree below the true energy of any example in the test set. Note that this model has
been validated in [219] for small distortions of the training set, where the maximum

errors were reported to be in the range of 1-2 kcal mol™!.

Moreover, a quick glance to the distribution of energies will reveal that the pre-
dictions of FFLUX are significantly detached from the real values. This is done in
Figure 3.8, where we report the energies as distributions around the mean, in or-
der to make them comparable. It can be seen that, while the test set is basically
a Gaussian distribution around the centre, FFLUX predicts an unrelated bimodal
distribution. More importantly, the minima of energy (which can be observed in
Figure 3.8, as the few examples in the left extreme of the histogram) are not ranked

correctly, but rather their energies are placed randomly within the distribution.

What is happening can be described as follows. In the 51-dimensional space
spanned by the features of capped glycine, the training set containing barely a thou-
sand examples is extremely sparse, and the interpolation of an arbitrary point in this
space yields a senseless result. This is unobserved if one employs a test set that is
very similar to the training set, as is usually done during parametrization tests, but

occurs clearly when one attempts to perform simulation.

These results show that the prediction of the kriging models is far less ideal
than one would expect. In the multiple assessments realised until now [234, 217,

216, 214], the test set is built with the same method as the training set, creating a


=====[PDF 114 | printed p. 113]=====
3.5. CONCLUSIONS 113

selection bias that fails to evaluate rigorously the performance of the methodology.
Moreover, the published simulation experiments [218, 219] show that the optimised
molecules reach the expected minimum, but not whether the energetics during the

trajectory are accurate or even sensible.

Additionally, the sampling concerns only one of the many possible conformers
of glycine. It is well-known that the amount of possible conformers grows expo-
nentially with the number of rotatable bonds [247]. Considering that the current
model requires 0.5 GB of examples to predict the energetics of one conformer only,
the size of the model required to explore the full conformational space will be very
large. This is not only important in terms of the tens of thousands of exponentials
that will be evaluated for every predicted magnitude in every atom — it will also

imply a significant consumption of the available memory.

The curse of dimensionality is also the reason why the optimisation of larger
examples, such as capped alanine and capped serine, which were recently tested
thoroughly in our group, suffer spontaneous dissociation due to the extrapolation
collapse, as in a high-dimensional space it is very easy to leave the training set. Fu-
ture work should be devoted to retest the validity of kriging models against test
sets that are representative of the actual geometries found during a simulation.
Moreover, methodologies should be implemented to reduce the number of training
points as much as possible, in order to avoid a catastrophic exponential increase of

the computational resources during a simulation.

3.5 Conclusions

In this chapter, we have provided a comprehensive description of the Quantum
Chemical Topology Force Field. We have explained its conceptual basis, introduc-
ing non-parametric regression models for the valence potential, as well as high-
order multipolar electrostatics with flexible multipoles. We have also reported the
derivation of the atomic forces in some detail, and described the parametrization
pipeline.

We have then assessed some of the difficulties of the formulation, aiming to un-

derstand its limitations. This analysis considers some phenomena whose influence


=====[PDF 115 | printed p. 114]=====
114 CHAPTER 3. THE QUANTUM CHEMICAL TOPOLOGY FORCE FIELD

can be crucial for the upscaling of the methodology, and that should be kept in mind
for further developments. In particular, we have presented three main challenges,

represented both by physical inaccuracies and mathematical difficulties.

The first challenge is the neglect of polarisation, which stems from the fact that
FFLUX only includes intramolecular polarisation. Two molecules of the same na-
ture will present the same charge distribution in any environment, as long as they
share the same geometry. This is an important conceptual problem, and will make
it difficult for FFLUX to compete with force fields, such as AMOEBA, that include
a physical description of polarisation. We have shown that the neglect of polari-
sation in FFLUX can yield errors of about 20 kcal mol! in energy differences for
simple water clusters, and particularly that it wrongly predicts which configuration

is more stable.

The second challenge, which we named as extrapolation collapse, represents the
problems of extrapolating beyond the training set. We provided mathematical ar-
guments that, as the predicted geometry differs significantly from the examples in
the training set, the prediction tends to a constant value, the mean of the Gaussian
process, and therefore the derivatives are null. We demonstrated the importance of
this collapse in a simple Monte Carlo simulation of a water molecule in vacuum,

and argued for the difficulties in palliating this problem.

The third challenge is a well-known problem in statistics and machine learning,
but also in numerical analysis: the curse of dimensionality. As the dimensionality of
the problem increases, even massive datasets become sparse in comparison with the
extension of the space. This results in a significant challenge when combined with
the extrapolation collapse, because it is virtually impossible to sample the whole
space for systems with more than a handful of dimensions. We demonstrate that
the curse of dimensionality is present by showing that state-of-the-art models with

~ 0.5 GB of examples fail to predict sensible energies for glycine.

Overall, all this challenges constitute open problems in the development of FFLUX.
Further work should be addressed to understand them in more detail and develop

suitable solutions.


=====[PDF 116 | printed p. 115]=====
Chapter 4

Delocalization indices and bond order

HE following chapter is an example of how research often starts at a
given point and evolves in a completely unexpected dimension. Here
we analyse a magnitude from Quantum Chemical Topology, the delo-
calization index, that was initially studied as a possible magnitude to tie together
individual monomer kriging models, and soon acquired a dimension of its own as

an indicator of bond order.

We note that the research discussed in this chapter has recently been published
as an Edge Article in the journal Chemical Science; a copy of the article is provided
in Appendix A. Although this chapter provides a more extensive discussion, along
with some unpublished results and comments that were unfit for the publication,
there are also many features in common, in particular the same figures. We also note
that our! contributions to the paper include the vast majority of the calculations and
the totality of the analysis: it is therefore legitimate to include the contents of the

paper in this thesis.

The initial problem that we aimed to tackle was how to model the peptide bond
or, in general, a bond between two individual monomers in a macromolecule. In
the current formulation of FFLUX, individual molecules, and thus also monomers,
interact only through the electrostatic and dispersion potentials. In such scheme,

there is in principle no way to model covalent interactions, such as the peptide

ln this case by ‘our’, in pluralis maiestatis, | imply ‘my’: the contributions made by Carlos Out-
eiral, author of this thesis and first author of the article.

115


=====[PDF 117 | printed p. 116]=====
116 CHAPTER 4. DELOCALIZATION INDICES AND BOND ORDER

bond, because they cannot model the exchange energy that is essential for covalency.

In order to model macromolecules, we need to incorporate a new term into FFLUX.

There are several possible approaches to achieve an appropriate model of the
peptide bond. First, one might employ an appropriately parameterised harmonic
potential between the aminoacids. This would in principle ensure the cohesion of
the peptide chain, but it is undesirable, both conceptually and technically, to spoil
the careful development of FFLUX by introducing the same coarse potentials that
it aimed to supersede. On the other hand, we might do just the opposite: introduce
a potential that explicitly includes the IQA exchange-correlation energy, which we

defined in Section 2.4.2 to be:

Vac(A,B) = I P1(11)P2(t2) — P2(t1, 12) dridry (4.1)

There are several problems in this formulation. Computing V;- requires a costly
six-dimensional integration in a complicated boundary; this also requires important
approximations, such as Miiller’s approximation described vide infra or the ones
discussed in Section 2.4.3. Moreover, since we are fitting a potential dependent on
the coordinates of two aminoacids, the model will require an enormous amount of
points due to the curse of dimensionality. Concerns of kriging performance aside,
the process of generating a training set with the full exchange-correlation energy

seems too costly to be reasonable.

A possible solution passes by approximating the exchange-correlation term by
another magnitude: the delocalization index. It was proved by Popelier for Hartree-
Fock theory that the delocalization index is a first order approximation to Vy¢ [248]
that requires only three-dimensional integrals; we will actually generalise this result
to arbitrary wavefunctions in Section 4.1.2. Moreover, this magnitude has been
applied in several theoretical studies, and is generally accepted as a bond order

indicator within the Quantum Chemical Topology community [249].

Prior to testing the performance of the delocalization index on exchange models,
we attempted to carefully analyse its behaviour in simple organic systems. This

study soon acquired a dimension of its own, revealing the interesting properties


=====[PDF 118 | printed p. 117]=====
117

and applications of delocalization indices, both as a bond order indicator and a
sign of covalency, and producing the rich variety of results that we will present in
this chapter. In fact, we will only discuss these properties, and leave the suitability

of delocalization indices for exchange potentials as future work.

Before proceeding to the exposition, it is valuable to reflect on why is it interest-
ing to study a bond order indicator. The answer can be summarised in a sentence:
although theoretical chemistry is traditionally in charge of developing concepts that
can make sense of chemical phenomena, one of such concepts, the chemical bond,
is still a significant matter of debate in the literature. The reasons of this conflict are

both historical and technical, and deserve to be considered carefully.

The chemical bond is traditionally understood in terms of the formulation pro-
posed by Gilbert N. Lewis in 1916 [250], that perceives a chemical bond as a pair
of electrons that is shared between two atoms. This vision was, however, brought
forth before the general advent of quantum mechanics, and reconciling the early
vision of shared electron pairs with wavefunctions has been a classical problem in

theoretical and computational chemistry.

Most of the attempts to reconcile both pictures have passed through electron-
counting arguments, such as those familiar from introductory chemistry. In those,
bond order is identified as the difference of electrons localized in bonding and an-
tibonding orbitals. However, this concept is of little use in many ordinary appli-
cations of quantum chemistry, for example when a single electron configuration is
a poor approximation to the electronic structure, or in periodic systems, where the

plane wave nature of the basis functions challenges the orbital interpretation.

Although many attempts have been made to modernise these arguments [251,
252], there is to date no universally accepted bond order indicator. In a way, the
theoretical object introduced in this chapter attempts to reconcile Lewis’ picture
with modern electronic structure in a different way, by recognising the concept of a
pair of delocalized electrons within the wavefunction, employing the formalism of

Quantum Chemical Topology presented in Section 2.4.

We introduce the concept of delocalization index in Section 4.1, as well as its re-

lation to the IQA formalism and the approximations usually employed to compute


=====[PDF 119 | printed p. 118]=====
118 CHAPTER 4. DELOCALIZATION INDICES AND BOND ORDER

it. Later, in Section 4.2 we analyse a body of almost 200 molecules, showing how to
extract chemical insight from these values. Finally, Section 4.3 extends the scope of
the study to some exotic bonding patterns, and Section 4.4 gathers the main conclu-

sions of this work.

4.1 Theoretical background

4.1.1 Delocalization indices

In order to arrive to the concept of delocalization index in a sensible manner, we
will state our objective and then manipulate the basic tools of quantum chemistry
to arrive at it. Since we want to reconcile modern electronic structure theory with
the Lewis’ picture of chemical bonding, we aim to find an indicator that yields the

amount of delocalized electrons among two atoms.

We will adopt the QTAIM scheme to partition the IR* space in a set of atomic
regions, although we note that any exhaustive partitioning of real space can be
used to calculate delocalization indices. We will also employed the reduced density

matrices of first and second order:

The terms A(A) and 6(A,B) are known respectively as the localization and de-
localization indices. From their definition, it is evident that the electron pairs of an
atom can thus be split into two subsets: those pairs that are unshared, localized in
the atom, and those that are shared or delocalized between that atom and the rest.
This allows to link the Lewis picture of electron sharing with electron population

analyses built on wavefunctions.

As mentioned in the introduction, delocalization indices are hardly new to the
QCT community; the concept was introduced by Bader and Stephens as early as
1975 [253], and its connection with the Lewis’ model has already been suggested
for covalent molecules [254], although with some problems’. DIs have been used in
the literature for some time [255, 256, 257, 258, 259], and there exist some general-
isations such as the n-order DIs [260, 261] and localization-delocalization matrices
[262, 263]; a particularly beautiful application is the monitoring of the delocaliza-
tion indices along a reaction path, that provides information about the nature of the
underlying chemical change [264, 265, 266, 267]. In fact, Fulton already proposed
in 1993 an expression for bond order that is equivalent to the delocalization index

computed within Miiller’s approximation described below [268].

We note that everything discussed to this point is experimentally observable in
principle. Since the exchange-correlation density is the expectation value of a Her-
mitian operator (in the theoretical jargon, it is a Dirac observable), then according
to the postulates of quantum mechanics it can be measured with some experimen-
tal setup. One of the strengths of our approach is that delocalization indices might

be experimentally measured in the near future; unfortunately, there is no reliable

3In particular, Fradera et al. in reference [254] comment that there are many cases where de-
localization indices cannot be directly identified with Lewis’ bond orders; for example, in the LiF
molecule we find 6(Li, F)=0.18 at the Hartree-Fock level of theory, which does not imply a bond
order of 0.18 but rather a more complex behaviour. We discuss these concerns in section 4.2, where
we understand the delocalization index as an insightful tool to analyse bonding, rather than an
indicator exactly equal to the often unreal Lewis bond order.

experimental methodology to determine pair densities to date, although there are

some interesting proposals from the field of quantum crystallography [269].

4.1.2 Connection to the IQA formalism

A very interesting feature of delocalization indices is that they are related to the
exchange-correlation energy Vxc in the Interacting Quantum Atoms (IQA) formal-
ism presented in Section 2.4.2. Since this magnitude is often used as an indicator
of covalency, it is reasonable to expect that another magnitude that represents bond

order is deeply related to the first.

We prove here that the delocalization index is a first-order approximation to Vy¢
in an arbitrary computational method. We start by considering the Hartree-Fock

pair density:

There are a few comments to be made about this derivation. Although we have
employed the multipolar expansion in spherical tensor form to ease the notation
and derive the result rigorously, its physical meaning might be slightly obscured.
The reader should not be distracted from the fact that we are simply performing a
Taylor expansion and truncating to the first term. In fact, if one chooses to approx-
imate the denominator in Vxc(A, B) as 1/R (to be compared with equation 2.52 in

section 2.2.3), the same conclusion will follow.

In a different view, the delocalization index can be understood as the interaction
between two exchange charges. A study of the behaviour of the multipolar expansion

of the exchange interaction has been recently performed by the Pendas group [271].

Finally, we note that the delocalization index is significantly cheaper computa-
tionally that the full exchange-correlation interaction. All the integrals in equation
4.17 are 3D integrals that can be computed readily, unlike the 6D integrals in equa-
tion 4.12 that can be very burdensome. In this way, the DI is a promising quantity
that could allow to understand exchange-correlation interactions in large systems,

where calculating the full Vxc is prohibitive.

4.1.3 The Miller approximation

Many of the derivations regarding QCT have retained a high degree of generality

through the formalism of reduced density matrices. However, as seen in equation

computing the }4;;,; elements.

The same problem does not appear in first-order reduced density matrices, that
are usually diagonalised and expressed in terms of their eigenvectors and eigenval-

ues, the natural orbitals Xj and occupation numbers vj:

4.2 Results and discussion
4.2.1 Computational details

Unlike the other chapters in this thesis, the research presented here is not related
to methodology development. We have not proposed any method or implemented
any code; rather, we have performed a large amount of quantum chemicals calcu-

lations, and carried out a large scale analysis of the results.

We have performed electronic structure calculations on ~ 200 molecules, both
organic and inorganic, covering the majority of most typical functional groups in or-
ganic chemistry. These comprise saturated and unsaturated (including several aro-
matic) hydrocarbons, haloalkanes, alcohols, aldehydes, ketones, acids (and deriva-
tives), amines, sulphides, several heterocyclic derivatives (of pyrrole and thiophene,
as well as the nitrogenated bases), cyanides and isocyanides, ethers, diazocom-
pounds and nitro-derivatives, and additionally the most common amino acids. This
set also contains 28 simple inorganic molecules, setting what is, to our knowledge,
the most complete delocalization index study to date. A comprehensive list of com-

pounds is provided in Appendix A.2.

The calculations were carried out with GAUSSIAN 09 [235], using the Pople
6-311++G(2d,2p) basis set, and either the Hartree-Fock or CCSD (Coupled Clus-
ter theory with Single and Double excitations) levels of theory. The majority of
reported data corresponds to Hartree-Fock theory, which will be assumed unless
otherwise stated. The calculated wavefunctions were then employed to calculate
QTAIM descriptors, including the delocalization index between each pair of quan-
tum atoms, employing AIMAII [236].

The output of GAUSSIAN for correlated wavefunctions is the set of natural or-
bitals, the eigenvectors of the 1-RDM [161]; no information about the 2-RDM is
outputted. For this reason, and for the computational expense and complexity of
correlated 2-RDMs, AIMAII employs the Miiller approximation discussed above in
the calculations. We provide a benchmark below, that employs the pySCF suite
[274] to obtain the full 2-~-RDM and the PROMOLDEN code [275] which can handle
the former. We note that the 6-311++G(2d,2p) basis set is not included in pySCF, so


=====[PDF 128 | printed p. 127]=====
4.2. RESULTS AND DISCUSSION 127

it was obtained from EMSL Basis Set Exchange [276].

Finally, we note that a detailed account of this results may be found in the Elec-
tronic Supplementary Information of the official publication.

4.2.2 Benchmark of Miiller approximation

Several articles have compared Miiller approximation with the full 2-RDM in a va-
riety of contexts [264, 277, 278, 279, 280]. In this section, we provide a short bench-

mark on 21 small molecules, which is provided in Table 4.1

In the original paper, Miiller stated that the complications of his approximation
may be compensated by the mean accuracy of this approximation for large N [273]. In this
sense, it is to be expected that the approximation will become asymptotically better
as the system size increases, and our benchmark can be interpreted as an upper

bound in the error.

The results in Table 4.1 show that Miiller’s approximation work best when cor-
relation plays a minor role on the system. Most of the DIs for directly bonded atoms
show relative errors below 5%, which is more than acceptable in relation to the enor-
mous computational savings. We note that the computation required 2 weeks in a
12-CPU node? for the full 2-RDM, versus ~ 10 hours in a 4-core node employing
AIMAIL

Unfortunately, the errors can be considerably large when correlation is impor-
tant. Strong examples of this are N2 and HCN, where the lack of static correla-
tion causes a relative error over 10%. Similarly, the delocalization indices between
atomic basins that are not directly bonded, which cannot be described correctly in
absence of dynamic correlation, show errors in the range 20-70% and always neg-
ative. An exception is posed by hydrogen bonds which, as shown in Table 4.1, are
overestimated by the Mller approximation, and generally by a significant amount.

There are two interesting exceptions: the molecules of F) and HF. The first is

well-known to pose difficulties at conventional levels of theory: it is unbounded
