Overall, this experiment shows that, without further computational effort, the
incorporation of symmetry in the kriging models enables a significant power of
extrapolation. If we assume that a simple kriging model requires to add all the
permutations of the training set, this simplification allows to reduce the number of

points six-fold, with impressive savings in computational time.

5.5 Conclusions

In this chapter, we have introduced and derived symmetry-adapted kriging: a
reformulation of Gaussian process regression that allows to incorporate symmetry.
This advance is, for many reasons, an important milestone in the development of
FFLUx. In the first place, it ensures that the simulations are consistent with molec-
ular symmetry, avoiding erratic behaviour due to symmetry breakings. Even more
importantly, it allows to significantly reduce the number of evaluations during a
simulation, suggesting that larger system sizes might be accessible in the near fu-

ture.

The formulation of symmetry-adapted kriging relies in the concept of faithful
representation: a set of matrices that behave like the elements of the group. Ina
way, this set ensures that we can represent the symmetry elements in an amenable
form in terms of matrices. Employing this matrices, we propose a functional form
for the correlation functions in kriging that is invariant to symmetry, and employ

elementary finite group theory to prove this invariance.

Although this formulation, by itself, does not allow to reduce the number of
evaluations during a simulation, we introduce a clever heuristic to save compu-
tational resources. This heuristic partitions the conformational space in a set of
symmetry-equivalent regions, and introduces a procedure to evaluate to which re-
gion does a point belong to. Moreover, the matrices of the faithful representation
allow to project points in one region to points in other regions. In this way, if we
model only one of the symmetry regions, we can provide an accurate prediction in

any of the other regions, simply by projecting it into the well-modelled region.

We provide several numerical verifications that this formulation is correct and

consistent. As a first test, we trained kriging models to reproduce only one of the


=====[PDF 182 | printed p. 181]=====
5.5. CONCLUSIONS 181

dissociation curves of H2O and NH3. We then confirmed that symmetry-adapted
kriging is capable of reproducing the dissociation of any of the bonds that is equiv-
alent by symmetry, while ordinary kriging can only reproduce the energy in the

conformational space it has been trained for.

Moreover, further experiments demonstrate that symmetry-adapted kriging is
consistent with the symmetry during a simulation. We have tested models of methanol
and acetaldehyde that contained examples of only one of its symmetry-equivalent
conformers. The results show that this formulation allows to extrapolate towards
unseen geometries employing only the symmetry group of the molecule. In all
cases, the geometries converge readily to the expected minimum within a negligible
RMSD. On the other hand, the baseline lacking ordinary kriging shows numerous

examples of unphysical behaviour due to the extrapolation collapse.

This advance will be incorporated in the next major version of FFLUX, and prob-

ably become one of the most powerful tools to reduce its computational cost.


=====[PDF 183 | printed p. 182]=====
Chapter 6

Conclusions

The current thesis presents several developments sprouted from the Quantum Chem-
ical Topology Force Field (FFLUX). This force field is a long-lasting endeavour
that aims to supersede classical molecular mechanics by introducing modern non-
parametric regression techniques that improve the valence and electrostatic poten-
tials. As comes with novel approaches, there are many open challenges that should
be addressed before the methodology is ready for deployment. The research pre-

sented here attempts to palliate these problems.

Our first development is motivated by the need of deploying inter-monomer po-
tentials for the simulation of macromolecules. In the current formulation of FFLUX
monomers interact only through electrostatics, but an exchange contribution is es-
sential to model covalent bonding, and therefore the peptide bond. Unfortunately,
it is not easy to build potentials that incorporate this magnitude: the models should
depend on the coordinates of both monomers, thus requiring a very large amount
of points. In addition, Vy: is a costly magnitude that requires the evaluation of

six-dimensional integrals, slowing down the development.

We have proposed the delocalization index, a magnitude from Quantum Chem-
ical Topology, as a suitable alternative. In order to understand its properties and
chemical meaning, we have done a theoretical analysis and performed a careful
study on about 200 compounds, both organic and inorganic. As we proved, the

delocalization index is a first-order approximation to the full exchange-correlation

182


=====[PDF 184 | printed p. 183]=====
183

energy in an arbitrary wavefunction, and can be computed much quickly since it
is a product of two three-dimensional integrals. It also reflects several chemical

phenomena, particularly the inductive and resonance effects.

The delocalization index is defined in terms of the second-order reduced density
matrix (or more rigorously its diagonal part, the pair density). Given the complexity
of an arbitrary correlated 2-RDM, a key step in our research was to validate Miiller
approximation: a Hartree-Fock-like estimate of the 2-RDM in terms of the 1-RDM.
Our benchmark showed that this approximation allows to compute delocalization
indices within a 10% error bound for directly bonded atoms, although larger errors

can be obtained when correlation is essential.

A large scale statistical analysis verified a fact suspected by the QCT community,
but never explored in detail: that the delocalization index is a bond order indicator.
We have shown that delocalization indices vary in a quasi-continuous fashion, but
cluster around certain well-defined values that can be identified with the classical
bond orders present in Lewis diagrams. We have also shown that there is a sharp
discontinuity between bonded and non-bonded terms, thus providing another evi-

dence of chemical bonding.

Finally, we attempted to extend the applicability of this approach by exploring
several exotic compounds. These included a Ng molecule, a compound with pla-
nar tetracoordinate carbon and a metallic complex whose actual bonding pattern
has been object of discussion in the literature. In all cases, we obtained sensible an-
swers, sometimes superseding other approaches in the literature. Specifically, we
discarded the presence of an ionic bond in Ng, that was suggested by NBO, and
provided a description of the metallic complex that is coherent with both hapticity
and electron-counting techniques.

Our second development is motivated by the curse of dimensionality. It is
a well-known result that, as the system dimensionality increases, the number of
points required to model its potential energy surface grows exponentially. Exploit-
ing the intrinsic symmetries becomes a necessary step to provide accurate models
of non-trivial molecules across their complete conformational space. Likewise the

neglecting of symmetry can yield dangerously unphysical behaviour, such as the


=====[PDF 185 | printed p. 184]=====
184 CHAPTER 6. CONCLUSIONS

case of methanol ignoring rotation barriers, which has been long studied by the

Popelier group.

We employ the tools of finite group theory to build a formulation of kriging that
is invariant to an arbitrary type of symmetry, which may or may not be point sym-
metry. This formulation relies heavily on the use of faithful representation matrices to
project a given configuration to a symmetric equivalent. Our derivation yields an
expression that ensures that kriging models are symmetric, but does not imply any
computational saving in principle. Fortunately, we are able to introduce a major

reduction of the points by proposing a heuristic algorithm.

Our algorithm splits the space of features in several disjoint regions equivalent
by symmetry, and employs the set of representation matrices to define mappings
between these regions. The kriging model is sampled only on one of the regions,
that is associated with the identity matrix. Whenever a prediction is done on some
features, the algorithm computes which region the point corresponds to, and maps
this point to the region that has been accurately modelled; the prediction process
proceeds as normal. This allows to incorporate symmetry to kriging models with

minor changes in the FFLUX implementation.

Although the symmetry-adapted kriging is general, to build the algorithm we
build on the concept of permutational symmetry: the invariance of a molecule (or
more rigorously, its Hamiltonian) to the permutation of atoms of the same type. For
example, permuting the labels of the hydrogen atoms in water does not alter any
property. In principle, FFLUX does not recognise permutational symmetry, since
the featurization system treats distinct atoms unequally, and the knowledge-based
nature of ordinary kriging makes it dependent on the order of the atoms. We show

that our formulation avoids these problems.

Simple experiments in water and ammonia demonstrate the invariance to sym-
metry of our formulation. Moreover, simulation experiments on methanol and
acetaldehyde show that modelling only one of the symmetry-invariant regions is
enough to provide optimisation capabilities in all the accessible conformational
space. Particularly, we demonstrate that the optimisations recognise and avoid ro-

tation barriers, a problem that could only be solved by massive sampling before


=====[PDF 186 | printed p. 185]=====
185

these developments.

Overall, our contributions have provided two major methodological improve-
ments. The first, the delocalization index, will be used as a bond order indicator
for chemical structure and reactivity studies; it might also become an useful quan-
tity for the Quantum Chemical Topology Force Field in the long run. The second,
symmetry-adapted kriging, is ready to be deployed as a tool to fit molecular poten-
tials, and can be incorporated in further versions of FFLUX.

delocalization measures in real space}

Carlos Outeiral, 2° Mark A. Vincent, 2° Angel Martin Pendas ®*°
and Paul L. A. Popelier ®*°

Cite this: Chem. Sci., 2018, 9, 5517

Ab initio quantum chemistry is an independent source of information supplying an ever widening group of
experimental chemists. However, bridging the gap between these ab initio data and chemical insight
remains a challenge. In particular, there is a need for a bond order index that characterizes novel
bonding patterns in a reliable manner, while recovering the familiar effects occurring in well-known
bonds. In this article, through a large body of calculations, we show how the delocalization index derived
from Quantum Chemical Topology (QCT) serves as such a bond order. This index is defined in
a parameter-free, intuitive and consistent manner, and with little qualitative dependency on the level of
theory used. The delocalization index is also able to detect the subtler bonding effects that underpin
most practical organic and inorganic chemistry. We explore and connect the properties of this index and
rsc.li/chemical-science open the door for its extensive usage in the understanding and discovery of novel chemistry.

Accepted 29th May 2018

DOI: 10.1039/c8sc01338a

A formalism meeting these requirements exists, and is
based on using measurable quantities (Dirac observables in
the language of quantum mechanics). Its conclusions are
guaranteed to be independent of modelling particularities (i.e.
what orbitals are used, what level of theory, and if the basis
functions are localized or delocalized). We apply the proba-
bility of finding electrons at a point in space, using the elec-
tron density or one-particle density, to define atoms. The
electron density leads to the fertile terrain of Quantum
Chemical Topology (QCT) as pioneered by the group of
Bader.*” Fig. 1 shows the result of such a real-space and

Theory

Quantum chemical topology is a branch of theoretical chem-
istry that employs the mathematical language of dynamical
systems (e.g. critical point, basin, attractor, separatrix) to
partition a quantum mechanical density function. When this
function is the electron density then one defines atoms within
a system. This is what the Quantum Theory of Atoms in Mole-
cules (QTAIM) achieves, as part of the QCT approach. Here, the
quantum mechanical density function is the electron density,
an important function legitimized by the Hohenberg and Kohn
theorems. Thus, one defines an atomic basin Q, also called
quantum atom for our purpose, as a subspace of R* bounded by
a number of interatomic surfaces S, each surface being defined
by the zero-flux condition or

After building a database of almost 200 compounds (accounting
for 14 756 entries of delocalization indices, only for Hartree-
Fock calculations), we can apply different analysis techniques to
scan for chemical insight in the delocalization indices. This
analysis focuses on the most abundant atom pairs in the study:
carbon-carbon (2364 data points), carbon-oxygen (632 data
points), carbon-nitrogen (724 data points) and carbon-
hydrogen interactions (5497 data points), and these are dis-
played in Fig. 2 and 3.

Delocalization indices in uncommon bonding patterns

In the previous section we have shown how delocalization
indices can contain a significant amount of chemical infor-
mation. However, our study has been limited to very well-
known molecules. Here, we take a step forward and apply
the knowledge, acquired in the previous part, to some
uncommon bonding patterns. We hope that this will pave the
way for a more widespread application of delocalization
indices and help towards the understanding of molecular
interactions.

1 Subroutine fflux_symmetry_transformation ()
! Symmetry transformation (COR)
: ! This subroutine applies the symmetry transformation

! to the cartesian coordinates.

Use kinds_f90
s Use config-module, Only : natms, xxx, yyy, zzz

Use fflux_module, Only : n_matrices, symmetry_matrices
10

1 Implicit None

13 Integer :: i, min-index, j

14 ~~ Real( kind=wp ), Allocatable :: cartesian_vectors(:, :)
is Real( kind=wp ), Allocatable :: distances (:), sorted_distances (:)
1 Real( kind=wp ) :: swap

is Allocate( cartesian_vectors(n_matrices, 3*natms) )

i  Allocate( distances(n-_matrices) )

2»  Allocate( sorted_distances(n_matrices) )

21

2 ! Set first cartesian vector (first matrix is identity)
2B Do i = 1, natms

24 cartesian_vectors(1, 3*(i—1) + 1) = xxx(i)

25 cartesian_vectors(1, 3*(i—1) + 2) = yyy(i)

26 cartesian_vectors(1, 3*(i—1) + 3) = zzz(i)

211


=====[PDF 213 | printed p. 212]=====
212 APPENDIX B. FORTRAN SUBROUTINES FOR FFLUX

2 End Do

2! Apply all matrix operations

3 Do i = 2, n_matrices

31 cartesian_vectors(i, :) = MAIMUL( symmetry-_matrices(i, :, :), &
32 & cartesian_vectors(1l, :) )

33 End Do

34

3s ! Compute distances with reference point

3 Call fflux.compute_distances(cartesian_vectors , distances)
37

3s min_index = MINLOC( distances, 1 )

39

40 ! Sort distances

41 sorted_distances(:) = distances (:)

2 Doi = 1, n_matrices

43 Do j = 1, n_matrices—i

44 If (sorted_distances(j) > sorted_distances(j+1)) Then
45 swap = distances (j+1)

46 sorted_distances(j+1) = sorted_distances (j)
47 sorted_distances (j) = swap

48 End If

49 End Do

5. End Do

51

52 ! If the difference is smaller than 0.1 choose the
53! minimum by default

54 If (sorted_distances(1)—sorted_distances(2) .le. 0.1) Then

55 swap = distances (min-index)
56 distances(min_index) = 1000.0
57 j = MINLOC( distances, 1 )

58 If (j .lt. min_index) Then

59 min_index = j

60 End If

6. End If

«6 Open( Unit = 25, Position = “APPEND”, Status = ‘OLD’, File = ”
FFLUX SYMMETRY. txt” )

« Write(25, *) min_index

oe ! Change cartesian vectors

70 yyy(i) = cartesian_vectors(min_index, 3*(i—1) + 2)
71 zzz(i) = cartesian_vectors(min-_index, 3*(i-—1) + 3)
7 End Do

73

1 Subroutine fflux_compute_distances(cartesian_vectors, distances)
3! Distances for symmetry transformation (COR)

5 Use kinds_f90
6 Use config-module, Only : natms

7 Use fflux_module, Only : n_matrices, reference_point_cart

9 Implicit None

10

un _Real( kind = wp ), Intent( In ) :: cartesian_vectors(n_matrices, 3x
natms )

2  Real( kind = wp ), Intent( Out ) :: distances (n_matrices)

3  Real( kind = wp ) :: Determinant

4

is  Real( kind = wp ) i: new _cartesian_vectors(n_matrices,
3*natms)

1 Real( kind = wp ) :: Q_matrix(natms, 3), P_matrix(
natms, 3)

7 Real( kind = wp ) :: centroid (3)

1s Real( kind = wp ) :: Q_vector(3*natms)

19 Real( kind = wp ) i: cross_cov(3, 3)

2 Real( kind = wp ) :: V_matrix(3, 3), W_matrix(3, 3),

S_matrix (3)


=====[PDF 215 | printed p. 214]=====
214 APPENDIX B. FORTRAN SUBROUTINES FOR FFLUX

2  Real( kind = wp ) :: Videt, W_det
2 Real( kind = wp ) :: rot-matrix(3, 3)
2 + Real( kind = wp ) >: WORK(100)

2 Integer 88 il, Jip IR

25 Integer :: IWORK(24)

2 Integer :: INFO

28

29 ! Create Q_matrix

» Do i = 1, natms

31 Q_matrix(i, 1) = reference_point_cart (3*(i—1)+1)
32 Q_matrix(i, 2) = reference_point_cart (3*(i—1)+2)
33 Q_matrix(i, 3) = reference_point_cart (3*(i—1)+3)
34 End Do

36 distances(:) = 0.0_wp

37

38 ! Compute centroid

39 Doi = 1, 3

40 centroid(i) = SUM(Q_matrix(:, i)) / natms

4 End Do

42

43 ! Substract centroid

44 Do i = 1, natms

45 Q_matrix(i, 1) = Q_-matrix(i, 1) — centroid (1)
46 Q_matrix(i, 2) = Q_matrix(i, 2) — centroid (2)
47 Q_matrix(i, 3) = Q_matrix(i, 3) — centroid (3)
4s End Do

49

5s. ! Rotate all symmetry—equivalent systems

51 Do i = 1, n_matrices

53 ! Create P matrix

54 Do j = 1, natms

P_matrix(j, 1) = cartesian_vectors(i, 3*(j —1)+1)
56 P_matrix(j, 2) = cartesian_vectors(i, 3*(j —1)+2)
57 P_matrix(j, 3) = cartesian_vectors(i, 3x(j —1)+3)
58 End Do

59


=====[PDF 216 | printed p. 215]=====
215

60 ! Compute centroid

61 Do j = 1, 3

62 centroid(j) = SUM(P_matrix(:, j)) / natms

63 End Do

64

65 ! Substract centroid

66 Do j = 1, natms

67 P_matrix(j, 1) = P_matrix(j, 1) — centroid (1)
68 P_matrix(j, 2) = P_matrix(j, 2) — centroid (2)
69 P_matrix(j, 3) = P_matrix(j, 3) — centroid (3)
70 End Do

71

72 ! Compute cross—covariance matrix

73 cross_cov = MAIMUL(TRANSPOSE(P_matrix), Q-matrix)
74

75 ! Compute optimal rotation matrix — LAPACK call
% Call dgesdd(’A’, 3, 3, cross_cov, 3, S_matrix, V_matrix, 3, &
7 & W-matrix, 3, WORK, 100, IWORK, INFO)
78 If (INFO .ne. 0) Stop

80 V_det = Determinant(V-_matrix)

81 W-_det = Determinant (W-_matrix)

82

83 If (V_det*eW_det .1t. 0.0) Then

84 V_matrix(:, 3) = —1 * V_matrix(:, 3)

85 End If

86

87 rot_matrix = MAIMUL(V_matrix, W_matrix)

88

89 ! Apply rotation matrix

90 Do j = 1, natms

91 P_matrix(j, :) = MAIMUL(rot-matrix, P_matrix(j, :))
92 End Do

93

94 ! Return to cartesian_vectors

95 Do j = 1, natms

96 new-cartesian_vectors(i, 3*(j—1)+1) = P_matrix(j, 1)

97 new-cartesian_vectors(i, 3*(j—1)+2) = P_matrix(j, 2)

98 new-cartesian_vectors(i, 3*(j—1)+3) = P_matrix(j, 3)


=====[PDF 217 | printed p. 216]=====
216 APPENDIX B. FORTRAN SUBROUTINES FOR FFLUX

99 End Do

100

101 End Do

102

103 ! Create a Q_vector with the form of a 3N cartesian vector

104 Do i = 1, natms

105 Q-vector(3*(i—1)+1) = Q_matrix(i, 1)

106 Q_vector(3*(i-—1)+2) = Q_matrix(i, 2)
107 Q_vector(3*(i—1)+3) = Q_matrix(i, 3)
108 End Do

109

uo! Now that Kabsch transformation is done, compute the

111 ! distances

12 Do i = 1, n_matrices

113

114 Do j = 1, natms

115 Do k = 1, 3

116 distances(i) = distances(i) + ( new-cartesian_vectors(i, 3*(j—1)+
k)—Q_vector (3*(j —1)+k) )**2

117 End Do

118 End Do

119

120 distances(i) = DSQORT(distances(i) / natms)

121

ix End Do

123
124

25 End Subroutine

uo End Function

1 Subroutine fflux_calc_cartesian(features, alf, iatm, cartesian)

Use kinds_f90
« Use config-module, Only : natms

s Use setup-module, Only : pi

7 Implicit None

9 Integer, Intent( In ) :: jiatm

1 Integer, Intent( In ) :: alf(natms, 3)

re Real( kind = wp ), Intent( In ) :: features (3*natms—6)
2 Real( kind = wp ), Intent( Out ) :: cartesian(3*natms)
B

us Integer :: i, nonalf(natms), j, temp
is Real( kind = wp ) :: xxx (natms)

1  Real( kind = wp ) 2: yyy (natms)

7 Real( kind = wp ) i: zzz(natms)

is Real( kind = wp ) :: r, theta, phi

1 Logical :: switch

20

2 ! ALF-defining atoms

2  xxx(alf(iatm, 1)) = 0.0_wp
2  yyy(alf(iatm, 1)) = 0.0-wp
2  zzz(alf(iatm, 1)) = 0.0_wp

2%  xxx(alf(iatm, 2)) = features (1)
2”  yyy(alf(iatm, 2)) = 0.0_wp

Appendix C

Derivation of multipolar electrostatics

In this appendix, we provide a derivation of the formulae for the interaction be-
tween multipoles up to quadrupole. These formulae have been employed in Chap-
ter 3, and are useful for pedagogic purposes.

C.1 Interaction between charge distributions

We begin by considering two distributions of charge, p;(r) and p2(r), defined
in regions of real space sufficiently disjoint to ensure that the conditions discussed
in Section 2.2.2 hold. The interaction energy between these two distributions in

vacuum, assuming that they do not alter each other, will be (in atomic units):

pil Tj )p2( rj) Te.
I I. |R+4;-1;]] —x;|| arid; (C1)

Here R is the vector joining r; and rj, the centers of both charge distributions; this
allows to express each charge distribution in its local frame of reference. Generally,
the integration over r; and 1; will be very complex, since the integration domain is
six-dimensional and the functional form of the distributions p; and p2 can be very
complex. However, it is possible to separate variables, as done in Section 2.2.3, by
expanding the term ||R + rj — r;|| tina Taylor series about r; — r;, which we denote

as Laplace or multipolar expansion:

C.4 Differences between quadrupole definitions

We note that the multipoles that we have introduced are the actual moments of
the electronic distribution; for example, we define the elements of the quadrupole

as:

on =f rr o(r) dr (C.37)


=====[PDF 231 | printed p. 230]=====
230 APPENDIX C. DERIVATION OF MULTIPOLAR ELECTROSTATICS

However, there are many other definitions. For example, Stone [81] defines the

quadrupole as:

ap _ 3 0,6 12
Oo; I (3+ Wor op) e(r) dr (C.38)

where r = ||r||. These are known as Buckingham’s traceless multipoles [331].

Perhaps unsurprisingly, these two different definitions can coexist. The reason

is that the Coulomb kernel Rj; ! satisfies Laplace equation:

It can then be seen that:

ye VaVe (a) =) Taa(R) = 0 (C.40)

The consequence of the equation above is that it is possible to modify the diago-
nal terms of the quadrupole by an arbitrary quantity without affecting the potential.
Hence, equations C.37 and C.38, with the only difference that the last also includes

the 1/n! term from the Taylor expansion.

C.5  Multipole rotation

Besides the expression for multipolar interactions derived above, there is an ad-
ditional step necessary for an accurate treatment of electrostatics. Multipole mo-
ments are tensorial magnitudes, that depend on the frame of reference, and it must
be ensured that they are placed in an adequate system of coordinates to obtain the

right results.

The system of coordinates in which the molecular positions are expressed is
often known as the laboratory frame, while the set of axes in which the multipoles are
defined is known as the local frame of a given atom or molecule. The transformation

formulas for dipoles and quadrupoles are:


=====[PDF 232 | printed p. 231]=====
C.5. MULTIPOLE ROTATION 231

yw =Cu (C.41)

6’ = cect (C.42)

Here, C is a rotation matrix, an orthogonal matrix with determinant +1 that ro-
tates the local frame axes into the laboratory frame axes, and the prime indicates the
laboratory frame multipoles. Note that the charge is independent of the system of
coordinates, since it is a scalar magnitude. Since C T — C-1 it is not uncommon to
see transposed and untransposed matrices interchanged, for a rotation matrix that

rotates the laboratory frame axes into the local frame axes.

The definition of the rotation matrix C employed in FFLUX is given towards the
end of Section 3.1.2.
